# 04 — Architecture Decisions

Each decision is recorded with its **reason** and its **consequences**. These are the foundations: getting them wrong would cost rewrites.

---

### D01 — Platform: PC first
- **Reason**: rich interface (chats, maps, management), online community, simple distribution (Steam, early access), freedom to update. Consoles: access barrier (dev kits, certifications); mobile: screen too small, wrong audience.
- **Consequences**: also playable with a controller from the start (Steam Deck, a Switch port possible one day).

### D02 — Visual style: isometric
- **Reason**: readability of towns, roads, constructions and armies; a natural grid for building.
- **Consequences**: choice between pure 2D and light 3D with a fixed camera and painted rendering, to be settled according to the graphics solution (probably AI). Visual consistency is the main challenge.
- **Settled on 6 November 2026: pure 2D isometric**, a 2:1 diamond grid of one-metre cells (64 × 32 px at scale 1), the ground from top-down textures projected and blended by the engine, elements as flat images standing on it, relief later as whole levels. A relief ground in 3D was weighed and declined: it buys continuous hills and costs the grid everything else rests on. The format every image obeys is in `09-visual-production.md`; the style in `07-style-bible.md`.

### D03 — Client: Godot (GDScript)
- **Reason**: free under the MIT license with no revenue condition, suited to 2D and stylized 3D, mature, readable text files (efficient with AI).
- **Consequences**: the client only **displays** and **transmits the player's intentions**.

### D04 — Simulation server: Rust, custom-built
- **Reason**: performance for a continuous simulation of tens of thousands of entities; a strict compiler that acts as a guardrail for AI-written code; total control (accelerated time, granularity). SpacetimeDB ruled out: non-free license, designed for queries rather than a heavy continuous simulation.
- **Consequences**: two languages at the heart of the project. Side services (accounts, website, tools) possibly in TypeScript.

### D05 — The server is the only source of truth
- **Reason**: anti-cheat, a world that lives without connected players.
- **Consequences**: the client decides nothing. The hidden rules (the Emperor's tree, karma) exist **only on the server**, never in the installed game files.

### D06 — Simulation separated from display, from day one
- **Reason**: going online must never require a rewrite.
- **Consequences**: even the first version runs as a Godot client connected to a local Rust server.

### D07 — A single persistent world, divided into regions
- **Reason**: wars, convoys and politics require a continuous world; locality (like EVE's solar systems) allows the load to be spread.
- **Consequences**: at first, everything fits on one machine; the region structure allows spreading later. If the population demands it, we open **another world** rather than expanding indefinitely.

### D08 — Interest management
- **Reason**: each player only receives what happens around them (performance, network).
- **Consequences**: reinforces the principle that information travels physically.
- **Status** (5 November 2026, step 1b): built at chunk granularity. Each connection holds a view of 19×19 chunks (608 m across, 9 November 2026; it was 5×5, then 9×9, then 11×11, then 13×13, then 15×15: the faster generation and loading of the performance audit paid for it, about 11 s of loading without a window, 480 MB on the client, the frames unchanged since only what is on screen is drawn) around its body. The client's view reaches **288 tiles from the body** either way, as far as those chunks always reach, and moves with it **tile by tile** (the chunks held move by whole chunks, the view does not: the lead's call, smooth and natural). Its edge is a fog in three parts, named by the lead so they can be tuned by name (`client/world/world_view.gd`), from the inside out: **fog C**, 15 tiles where a veil of the fog's own colour starts to rise over the **ground only**, what stands (trees, plants, items, bodies) showing over it; **fog B**, the view's last 35 tiles, where the veil keeps rising over **everything**, whole at the limit; **fog A**, 5 tiles of fog past the limit with nothing drawn under it, in soft drifting banks. The fog is a soft grey (a white one was too loud) and 70 % thick at most, so the land past the limit is still guessed through it; the land keeps its colours and melts into it. The view's corners are rounded (64 tiles at the limit, the inner fogs' arcs sharing their centre): a square corner looked odd. The **camera stops** at fog A's end: no corner of the screen passes it, so pushing to an edge shows the fogs and never what lies beyond, and walking that way moves them along (the lead's calls, 9 November 2026, tried live: a ring of 2 outline chunks fading over 64 tiles showed too much; 3 tiles blurring past the chunks held with the fog barely seen did not tell where the view ends; darkening the land under the veil was not what was meant; the veil on the ground only let what stands show over it, which the lead liked; 3/6/20 tiles of fog and 3/10/20/30 of veil were tried before 3, 9 and 15, then 20, 20 and 15, then 5, 35 and 15; the ring of outline chunks, protocol v15 `chunk_outline`, is gone in v16, so the client holds only the 19 × 19 chunks). **The view of the land and the range of the creatures are to be two distances** (the lead agreed, 9 November 2026): the land, still and cheap once loaded, is sent out to the view; the creatures (animals, people, players) that move only within what the body perceives (D18), a smaller range, so that many of them do not cost each client in proportion to the view's area. Under the fog the client still draws the **far view** (protocol v14, `far_view`): the land 2 km around the body, one letter a cell of 8 tiles (grass, forest, water, rock), sent after the first chunks and again when the body has gone 16 chunks from its centre, drawn under the chunks with a grain; the fog hides it now, and whether it stays is to be decided. The connection receives those chunks, each followed by the bodies standing in it (since protocol v17 a chunk is a binary frame and its bodies a message of their own, D26), then only the moves inside them; as the body walks, chunks entering the view are sent and chunks leaving it are hidden, and a body walking out is reported as left. Perception by line of sight, hearing and witnesses (D18) will refine this inside the view.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026 — the client builds only what the screen reaches** (lightness plan, C1 and C5). The 19 × 19 chunks a connection holds are still sent, kept and used as before: the terrain grid the borders read, the items within reach, the trees' and plants' data, the limit and the fog. But a chunk is **built** (its ground canvases, items, plants, shadows and trees given nodes and canvas items) only when the camera's reach, the screen and a chunk of margin around it, touches it: the chunks the screen may show (its rectangle grown by what may stand out of a chunk's ground, 1024 px up for the tallest trees) are built **the frame they enter the reach**, whatever else builds that frame, its water meshes built in line rather than on a worker so the water shows with the ground; the margin's build one a frame, nearest the camera first, and one the screen catches up with before its turn is built that frame after all. A built chunk the reach left is released **after a grace of 2 s**, its data kept, so it builds again from them with no network; the grace and the margin are the hysteresis: the camera trembling on an edge, or the body walking back and forth over one, builds nothing twice. The chunks hidden at a border crossing are no longer parked: a chunk hidden lies 9 chunks from the body, never in reach. Nothing changes on screen: the chunks built hang in the order the server sent them (where two chunks' drawings overlap at a border, and for two trees whose feet stand level), and the far view leaves its hole once the chunks the screen shows are built. Measured on the game's valley (`content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/vue-ecran/`): loading at connection **3.8–5.2 s → 1.0 s** without a window (3 alternated runs each), **10.7–13.7 s → 1.1–1.5 s** with one; a teleport's loading **10.5–17.3 s → 0.6–2.7 s**; nodes **4,894 → 300** in the forest at zoom 0.4 (8,952 → 1,271 at the forest's edge), memory **−33 to −42 MB**, video memory **−17 to −32 MB**; walking 20 tiles and back builds 3 chunks each way instead of 19 (and the parked 19 restored); a camera trembling on the spot builds nothing; a fast drag (400 tiles in 40 frames) builds about 70 grounds as it goes, its worst frame 30 → 45–90 ms (more crossing a river: the water meshes built in line, D36). Captures before and after: 24 of 29 identical to the pixel, the rest within the noise `main` shows between two of its own runs (the arrival order of chunks from the server's pool decides ties between trees whose feet stand level)..
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, later the same day — the grounds worked out on worker threads** (lightness plan, C3; D39). Building a chunk's ground on the frame it entered the reach cost 45 to 90 ms, and 150 to 185 ms with a river (its water meshes built in line): the picture jumped while dragging or walking. Now the reach's margin grows as the zoom pulls back (`ViewLedger.margin_for`: one chunk from zoom 1.5, two from 0.7, three below), so a chunk is built before the screen gets there; its ground (which canvas draws each tile, the forest's spill, the flat tiles, the decorations), its water meshes' arrays, its brooks' ribbons and its low plants are worked out on Godot's worker threads (`GroundBuild`) from what the main thread gathered (the codes around it, a copy of the currents near it), and the main thread only **lays** the result: it writes the ground index, makes the meshes from their arrays and keeps the draw commands, about 0.3 ms a chunk. The grounds done are laid nearest the camera first within 4 ms a frame; the margin's chunks get their nodes (trees, tall plants, items) nearest first within 3 ms a frame. This replaces "the frame they enter the reach" and "one a frame" above, except as a safety net: a chunk the screen catches before its worker is done waits for it on the main thread, so it still never shows without its ground or its water (counted in F3, "built in line"). Measured offscreen, the camera dragged 400 tiles in 40 frames over the river at zooms 0.4 and 1: the worst frame **155–185 ms → 31–34 ms**, the frame's own floor there being about 26 ms; 0 to 2 grounds waited for a drag, none a visible stall.
- **Amended on 19 November 2026 — the grounds wait their turn for the workers** (audit F6). Every ground went to the workers the moment its chunk was built or its neighbours came in, and a ground dropped meanwhile (its chunk released, or a newer one asked for) still ran to its end. After teleports at forced peak fruit, where a thicket's ground takes 0.5 to 7 s on a worker, the pool held dozens of grounds, many of them dropped, and the main thread, waiting for the screen's own behind them, finished no frame for 20 s: the dev watchdog's abort (the audit's SIGSEGV), 2 of 20 headless stress runs. Now at most half the processor's threads work on the margin's grounds (`ChunkView.builds_at_once`); the others wait their turn and are given out nearest the camera first. The screen's own (built at once, or reached before their turn: `ChunkView.hurry`) go to the workers at once, together, so the frame waits for them once. A ground drawn before its turn is worked out in line (the same, bit for bit). A ground dropped before a worker started it is skipped, and one dropped while waiting its turn costs nothing. When the world view leaves the tree (the game quits), every ground on the workers is waited for (`ChunkView.finish_all`), so no worker is still in a script while the engine shuts down. With this, the same stress (`SOUL_AUDIT_FRUIT=8`, teleports between the thicket and the forest at zooms 0.4 and 2.5, then quitting while grounds are under way) ran 28 times (and 30 more with two earlier variants of this change) without a stall, a hang or a crash, and quitting took 0.2 to 1.5 s (up to 6 s before). Alternated with the code before, 8 runs each, the worst frame of a run is 2.3–4.3 s against 2.3–7.5 s, and the time spent in frames over 2 s 35 s against 67 s. Nothing changes on screen. Those long frames remain at forced peak fruit: they are the cost of the grounds themselves.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, in the evening — never resend what the client holds** (lightness plan D4, protocol v18; the lead's calls of the same day). The client keeps in **memory only** (never on disk: it would be a map of everything the player saw, against "no built-in map") the raw frames of the chunks in view and of the **1,500 chunks last left behind** (about 3 MB; `FrameCache`), each with its version. The server **mirrors** that cache per connection by the same rule (the least recently hidden forgotten first, `protocol::CHUNK_CACHE_CHUNKS`) and, for a chunk coming back into view that it believes the client keeps at its current version, sends **`chunk_same {x, y, version}`** (followed by the bodies standing in it, as after a frame) instead of the frame. A wrong guess heals itself: the client answers **`chunk_missing {x, y}`** and gets the frame. The chunks leaving the view are no longer hidden one by one: each move of the view sends **one `view` message**, the window held (a rectangle of chunks), and the client lets go of what lies outside, keeping their frames in rows-then-columns order, as the mirror does (a teleport sent 361 `chunk_hidden`). `world_state` names its world (**`world`**, sixteen hex digits: a hash of the map's spec, of the kinds the frames name by index, and of the frames of five chunks as the seed made them, so that the same world generated by other code is another world; and, since the same night, of a stored world's birth and epoch, one more after each crash: D28); on a **reconnection to the same world** the client keeps its chunks and their nodes (the bodies forgotten: they come again with the chunks) and tells, in its **`hello`** (`held`, at most 2,048 entries: the one message allowed up to 64 KiB, every other still 4 KiB), the chunks it holds and their versions; the server sends `chunk_same` for those unchanged. In the hello rather than in a message after it, so the server knows them before its first chunk leaves. A client that lies about what it holds only misses what it would have been sent. A chunk's **version** is now exact: it counts every change to what its frame carries, an item taken, dropped, eaten or laid by a death as much as a natural item taken (D2 counted only the last; the server's own count of item changes is gone), so an equal version means an equal frame.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, in the evening — never resend what the client holds** (lightness plan D4, protocol v18; the lead's calls of the same day). The client keeps in **memory only** (never on disk: it would be a map of everything the player saw, against "no built-in map") the raw frames of the chunks in view and of the **1,500 chunks last left behind** (about 3 MB; `FrameCache`), each with its version. The server **mirrors** that cache per connection by the same rule (the least recently hidden forgotten first, `protocol::CHUNK_CACHE_CHUNKS`) and, for a chunk coming back into view that it believes the client keeps at its current version, sends **`chunk_same {x, y, version}`** (followed by the bodies standing in it, as after a frame) instead of the frame. A wrong guess heals itself: the client answers **`chunk_missing {x, y}`** and gets the frame. The chunks leaving the view are no longer hidden one by one: each move of the view sends **one `view` message**, the window held (a rectangle of chunks), and the client lets go of what lies outside, keeping their frames in rows-then-columns order, as the mirror does (a teleport sent 361 `chunk_hidden`). `world_state` names its world (**`world`**, sixteen hex digits: a hash of the map's spec, of the kinds the frames name by index, and of the frames of five chunks as the seed made them, so that the same world generated by other code is another world); on a **reconnection to the same world** the client keeps its chunks and their nodes (the bodies forgotten: they come again with the chunks) and tells, in its **`hello`** (`held`, at most 2,048 entries: the one message allowed up to 64 KiB, every other still 4 KiB), the chunks it holds and their versions; the server sends `chunk_same` for those unchanged. In the hello rather than in a message after it, so the server knows them before its first chunk leaves. A client that lies about what it holds only misses what it would have been sent. A chunk's **version** is now exact: it counts every change to what its frame carries, an item taken, dropped, eaten or laid by a death as much as a natural item taken (D2 counted only the last; the server's own count of item changes is gone), so an equal version means an equal frame.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, late in the evening — interest by place** (lightness plan D5 and A6, protocol v19; the range is the lead's call of the same day). The view of the land and the range of the creatures are now two distances, as agreed on 9 November: the land is still sent out to the 19 × 19 chunks held, the **bodies only within 150 m** of the client's body, measured between the centres of the chunks they stand in (a disc of chunks, four either way along the axes: a body is seen within about 150 m, never past 192 m; `server::interest::BODY_RANGE_M`). A watcher without a body has the same range around the middle of its view. A chunk's frame is followed by its bodies (`chunk_bodies`) only within that range; as the body walks, a chunk held that comes within range has its bodies told (`chunk_bodies` again, after its frame) and the bodies known that now stand out of it are told left (`body_left`). Events are **routed by place**: each connection tells the server's router (`server/server/src/interest.rs`) the chunks it holds and the chunks within its range; an item's change goes to the connections holding its chunk, a body's to those whose range covers the chunk it stands in and to its own pilot wherever it is, the day and the night to all. What a connection is handed follows what stands around it, never the world's population (it was handed every step of every body in the world and threw away what lay outside its view). A walking body is **published when it walks into another chunk**, not at each tile (about 22 times fewer: a chunk is 32 tiles), with its place and its route: a client within range is told `body_walks` again; a body walking into range is announced the same way, with its route. The client walks a body **by time**, not at the nominal speed: `body_walks` and the bodies of `chunk_bodies` carry the tick each waypoint is reached (`arrivals`), worked out by the simulation with its own steps (`sim::timeline`, again at each chunk), the route holding the points where the pace changes with the ground; between two of them the body walks straight at one pace, so the client shows it where the world has it, in a forest as on grass, and each message agrees with what is shown. A body told within 3 m of where it is shown walks on from there to its next waypoint on time; farther (a new order, a refusal), it stands where it is told (`client/world/body_walk.gd`). (12 November 2026: a body told within 3 m now walks its route as the world has it, the gap to where it was shown closed evenly at half its pace at most, before the walk ends. Heading straight for the next waypoint on time made a dash, back about a tile and on, when a new destination was clicked while walking: the client's clock a few ticks off the world's, the new route's first waypoint, near where the world turned the body, lay behind where it was shown, and was due within a tick or two.) The reason: at the nominal speed, the client ran metres ahead of a body slowed by a forest and pulled it back at each chunk. The server still records every step (`Shared::body_moved`), so a body told is told where it is. The reason: a world of thousands of walking creatures made every connection wake for each of their steps and fall behind; now a connection's work and what its client receives follow the bodies near it.
- **Amended on 19 November 2026 — the far view on a grid, the frame cache read first** (step 4, network thinker's ideas 1 and 2a; no protocol change, nothing changes on screen). The far view is no longer centred on the body's own chunk but on the middle chunk of the body's square of a **16-chunk grid** (`net::far_centre`, `FAR_GRID`); it is still sent again once the body is more than 16 chunks from the centre it was sent with. Its cells lie on the same 8-tile grid wherever it is centred, so a cell shows the same land in any far view (a test compares them), and the window still reaches at least as far past the camera's stop as before (at worst 480 tiles from the body, the camera stopping at 293). Counting a far view (≈ 300 ms on the pool) is now done once a square, shared by every player in it while the far views held keep it, the same bytes for all. And the pool reads a chunk frame's cache key (the ground's version, then the items' version and the fruit phase) **before** making the chunk's ground: a frame already held is sent without making anything again (its natural items alone cost a millisecond a chunk); should the key move before the frame leaves, the chunk is built again whole. A warm login's 361 chunks cost the pool 0.6–0.75 s → under 1 ms (measured on the valley, alternated, release).
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — what the eyes tell, and no more** (the security audit of 20 November 2026, its phase 2 approved by the project lead the same day; findings F2, F6, M1 and M2). What men do to the ground (`item_added`, `item_removed`) is told within the creatures' range (150 m, now `sim::EYES_RANGE_M`, the server's `BODY_RANGE_M`), no longer the whole view: a tile changing 200 m away told where a hidden body acted. The chunk's version moves all the same, and the chunk is sent again when it comes within range or at the next dawn. An act on an item is judged by the body's reach and eyes before anything is done or told of the item: within the eyes a refusal says why (`out_of_reach`, `not_carried`); past them it is `unknown`, whether the item lies there, is carried by someone or does not exist, and a far natural item is never made for a probe (it was: every probe wrote the item into the world and its chunk's version). A death's `cause` is told to the dead body's own pilot alone; a witness gets `body_died` without it, the field left out: a change of meaning in the protocol, not of shape (v30 kept; the client already read the field as optional).

### D09 — Adjustable time and a calendar specific to each world
- **Reason**: the world has its own calendar and its own biology; testing requires massive acceleration.
- **Consequences**: durations (day, season, childhood, lifetime) are adjustable independently and defined as world data. An accelerated mode without players is **mandatory** from the start.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — the year belongs to the world's calendar, and two clocks** (the vivarium plan, D41; the project lead's decisions of 12 and 13 November). The world now has a **year of four seasons**, provisionally about **50 game days** (about four real days with a two-hour day): a life is lived in months of real time, and a player who comes back after a real month finds people years older; the lore already says the planet turns fast. The value is adjustable and **must be fixed before a world opens** (`06`). The year is part of each world's calendar, beside the day, never a constant of the code; the length of the day varies with the season (content). **Two clocks**: what is measured in days follows the day (hunger, thirst, spoilage, retting), what is measured in years follows the season (pregnancy, growth, healing, harvest windows). Reason: a rule counted in the wrong unit breaks when the year is tuned; with two clocks, changing the year's length never changes how fast meat rots.
  - **Built on 13 November 2026 — the year and the seasons, in the clock only** (lot N, second piece). A world's `Calendar` holds its **year** beside its day: `days_per_year`, **50** by default (`--days-per-year`, at least 4, a day per season), stored with the world. Any tick has its **year** and **day of the year** (both from 1) and its **season**: the year is cut in four **at whole days**, day of the year (from 0) × 4 / `days_per_year`, so a season starts at dawn and a day belongs to one season only; seasons are equal when the year is a multiple of four, else as near as whole days allow (50 days: spring 13, summer 12, autumn 13, winter 12). Why whole days rather than exactly equal quarters in ticks: a day's length and its night are set by its season, and a season changing at noon would move the night within a day. **The length of the day follows the season** (content, `content/needs.ron`): night begins at a percent of the day by season, spring and autumn 58 (19:55), summer 64 (21:20), winter 56 (19:25). Winter is shortened only that much until cold by season, fire and shelter come: at 56 % a winter night outside costs a body about the vitality the day gives back, where 50 % would kill it within the season. At the dawn of each season the simulation tells `SeasonStarted` (year, season); the world's first spring begins untold, with the world. Nothing grows or cools by season yet. Protocol **v24**: the clock carries `days_per_year`, so a client knows the day, season and year of any tick (the HUD shows them beside the hour, from the tick); `season_started` goes to every client. Snapshot format **v12**: a world saved before gets the year of 50 days.
- **Amended on 24 November 2026 — three clocks, and the calendar v1** (the project lead, 24 November 2026, on the calendar's proposal and the engineer's report of the same day, both kept in the AI reserve: "je suis d'accord pour la proposition de temps"). The two clocks become **three**: the **day** (needs, gathering, walking, rot); the **age-year** (ageing, pregnancy, childhood, fertility, healing); the **cycle** of four seasons (nature: fruit windows, crops, tree growth). Age no longer turns with nature: a body ages several age-years in one cycle. **The v1 values**: a game day of **16,200 s** (4 h 30 real at the game's 10 ticks a real second); **16 game days a season**, so a cycle of **64 game days** (12 real days); an **age-year of 8 game days**, half a season, 8 age-years a cycle (36 real hours); night by season, **summer 25 %, spring and autumn 30 %, winter 38 %** of the day. Today the age-year *is* the cycle: 50 game days, days of 7,200 s. The lead's reasons: a 4 h 30 day is long enough to do things in, and its hour shifts each evening (by 1 h 30 a real day), so a player who plays at the same hour does not live the same time of day; a life is about **3 real months** (60 age-years, 480 game days), **240 age-years a real year**, and a played life nearer **2 real months**, since souls rarely take children and may die before old age; a player who comes each day lives a season over about **3 real days**; a player who comes at the weekend sees **a new season each week**. Reason for three clocks: tied to one year, a short life made nature race (a crop every 8 game days, winters of 2) and a long cycle made lives endless; apart, each keeps its own pace.
  - **Built on 24 November 2026 — the plumbing, runs identical (lot 1).** A world's `Calendar` carries its **age-years per cycle** (`age_years_per_cycle`) beside its day and its cycle (`days_per_cycle`, stored and sent under its first name, `days_per_year`). The cycle must be cut into **whole age-years of 4 days at least**, so that an age-year's quarters, like the seasons, fall at whole days. Any tick has its **age-year** and its **quarter of age-year** beside its cycle and its season. Ages (`World::age_milli`, and with it every `_years` field of the content: fertility, mating, woman, weaning, slowness, old age, following the mother, need and death by age), the daily death draw (the age-year's chance shared over the age-year's days), **pregnancy** (`pregnancy_quarters`, formerly `pregnancy_seasons`: quarters of an age-year, in whole days) and **conception** (one draw a quarter of an age-year, on its first `conceive_nights` dawns, seeded by the age-year and the quarter) follow the age-year; crops, fruit windows, the trials' horizons and a move's repayment in seasons, and the will's tempering stay on the cycle. With one age-year a cycle, the default, every number is the same as before: a vivarium run (seed 9, 250 days) is **byte-identical**, its data differing only by the new meta keys and the renamed factor. The vivarium takes `--seconds-per-day`, `--days-per-cycle` and `--age-years-per-cycle` (today's by default), and its data records them (`docs/10`); the lab reads the cycle and the age-year from the data. **Snapshot format v18**: a world saved before gets one age-year a cycle, the **legacy value pinned** (`Calendar::LEGACY_AGE_YEARS_PER_CYCLE`), never read from a default that may change; the 50-day cycle of v12's migration and the age of a body saved before births are pinned the same way. **No protocol change**: ages are not sent; showing the age-year in the HUD is a later lot. Left as they are, each for a decision: what the engineer's report found mis-tied for an 8-day *year* (`lesson_days`, `hearth_nights`, `fade_per_day`, `part_after_days`, `keeps_days`, `reserve_kj`, a crop's grams and lean years, the daily try chances, the trial days) moves only ×1.28 against a 64-day cycle; the chances per life are an open question (`06`); healing is counted per day (`vitality_regain_per_day`) though the lead's list puts it on the age-year; the will's tempering counts a season as a quarter of the cycle floored (12 days at 50, against seasons of 13 and 12; exact at 64). **The switch to the v1 values is the next lot (lot 2)**: the new defaults, the crop rule (grams × richness × days ÷ 365), then a control run and its report.
  - **In force on 24 November 2026 — the switch to v1 (lot 2)** (the project lead: "on peut lancer ça"). New worlds, the server's defaults and the vivarium's are the calendar v1: `Calendar::DEFAULT_SECONDS_PER_DAY` 16,200, `DEFAULT_DAYS_PER_CYCLE` 64, `DEFAULT_AGE_YEARS_PER_CYCLE` 8 (the server takes `--age-years-per-cycle` beside `--days-per-year`); `content/needs.ron`'s night begins at 70 % of the day in spring and autumn, 75 % in summer, 62 % in winter. The live server stays at 10 ticks a real second. Pregnancy and conception follow from lot 1 (6 game days; a draw every 2 game days, so about the same age-years between births: every life rule counted in age-years scales whole). A world saved before keeps its stored day and its pinned legacy cycle, 50 days and one age-year a cycle; the nights, being content, are v1's for it too. The crops follow the cycle by the rule of D45 (amended the same day): a plant's content keeps a real year's yield, the game gives it × the world's richness × the cycle's days ÷ 365. Settings still counted in days or in seasons are left as they are, each for the lead's decision (the list is in the pull request of lot 2 and in `06`): with a 64-day cycle they move by ×1.28 against it, and by ÷6.25 against an age-year.
  - **Decided on 24 November 2026 — the settings counted in days** (the project lead validated the table as it stood). What was kept "a year" (lessons heard at the fire, the hearth's nights, habits) is kept a cycle of the four seasons, 64 days; nuts, wild grain, peas and seeds keep a cycle at least, so autumn's food lasts to spring; the will and the confidences come back half their gap in two age-years (`relax_permille` 500 a season, a season being two age-years); `court_days` is renamed `together_nights` (no courtship: nights side by side; the old key is still read). A physical absence, the body's fat, a wound's healing and the day-scale behaviours keep their days; the daily chances to try stay open (docs/06). Storage and protocol unchanged.
- **Amended on 25 November 2026 — every long duration in its own unit** (the project lead, principle D of the clocks audit, `content/ai-reserve/review/vivarium/lot-5/audit-horloges-2026-11-25.md`, §2 point 8 and §3 row D). **Reason**: eleven settings written in days were a season or a cycle only by the coincidence of calendar v1 (16 days a season, 64 a cycle), against this decision's own rule of 13 November and D15's ("long durations are written in seasons"): a tuned calendar would have left them behind. **The rule**: a long duration is written in its own unit, never in days: **seasons** for what follows nature (a cycle being four), **age-years** for life (already so); it becomes days through the world's `Calendar` where it is used, seasons × the cycle's days ÷ 4 rounded down to whole days (the rule `horizon_seasons` already followed). **Moved, calendar v1's values kept exactly**: `trials.lesson_days` 64 → `lesson_seasons` 4; `fire.hearth_nights` 64 → `hearth_seasons` 4; `decisions.habit_days` 64 → `habit_seasons` 4; a food's `keeps_days` 64 → `keeps_seasons` 4 (hazelnuts, wild peas, wild grain, flax seeds, sweet chestnuts); folded birch bark's `wears_days` 64 → `wears_seasons` 4. A material keeps, or wears, in days **or** in seasons, never both (refused at load). **One value changed**: walnuts kept 90 days with no written reason (5.6 seasons) → `keeps_seasons` **6**, a cycle and a half (96 days in v1): the nearest whole seasons (80 or 96), still longer than the other nuts as the 90 meant. **Kept in days**, short, of the body and the day (the day's clock): the short keepings (1 to 30 days: leaves, berries, inner bark, roots, pears), `life.part_after_days` 12 (a physical absence, as decided on 24 November; its comment, "about a season" from the old 12-day season, now says three quarters of a season in v1), `together_nights` 6, `conceive_nights` 4, `trials.trial_days` 5 and `trial_after_days` 3, `home_nights_by_will`, `fire.told_days_per_hour` and `told_days_max` (bounded by the fruit's season already), `ages.care_days`, `reputation.fade_per_day`, `lived_keep_permille` and the needs' rates a day. The comment of `calm_try_permille_by_will` ("a year of fifty days") now counts a cycle of 64. A world of another cycle (the legacy one of 50 days) now keeps these a cycle of its own. **No change in v1, verified**: the vivarium's small run (2 days) and a couple's twenty age-years (160 days) give the same JSON report before and after, but for the three factors' names; the simulation's tests pass unchanged but for a hearth test now given a 4-day cycle, so that its one season is one night. Storage and protocol unchanged.

### D10 — Levels of detail in the simulation
- **Reason**: simulating everything in fine detail everywhere is impossible.
- **Consequences**: full detail near players, summarized simulation elsewhere, bulk simulation far away, with a return to the individual level when a player arrives. States (memories, opinions) are never lost.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — persons counted far from players, strong personalities named, kingdoms made at first contact** (the kingdom study, `content/ai-reserve/review/conception-du-jeu/royaume/`; the project lead's decisions of 12 November). Far from every player, the people of a community are **counted on a community sheet**, not simulated one by one; only its **strong personalities** (`03`) are named individuals with their own lives. A person a player has met **stays an individual once seen**: whoever comes back finds the same faces. A kingdom that nobody has ever approached is not built (the world lives, but what is not seen is not built): at its **first contact** (a visit, a raid, a merchant) it is **generated by running the same rules in isolation from the world's birth, then frozen** into the world. Reason: fitting unseen lands to their neighbours would spread the players' progress over the whole continent for free, like levels that follow the player; neighbours influence one another only through real contacts.

### D11 — Target: several tens of thousands of NPCs
- **Reason**: a living, populated world.
- **Consequences**: data organized for performance; thinking **spread over time**; optimized movement (precomputed paths, no pathfinding for distant NPCs); **relationships limited to known people** (~150); old memories summarized.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — what the count means** (the kingdom study; the vivarium plan's second check). The tens of thousands are the bodies simulated in fine or summarized detail; most of a continent's people are counted on community sheets (D10), with their strong personalities named, a thin share of the population, light enough to keep for a whole continent. The dead leave a **register** that keeps kinship. Reason: the names a player meets and the lines of descent must outlive the bodies, without simulating everyone.

### D12 — Willpower sets the richness of NPC thought
- **Reason**: lore, reincarnation cost and performance brought together in a single notion.
- **Consequences**: Willpower sets **how far** an NPC thinks; proximity to players sets **how often**.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — Willpower is the depth of search and the scope of choices** (the project lead's decisions of 12 November). "How far" is made precise twice. In discovery, Willpower is **how many steps back** from a need a mind searches the content for a way to meet it, among what it has to hand (`03`, vivarium V3). In choices, Willpower is **scope, not randomness**: a body always chooses what resembles it; a modest one makes small, routine choices, a strong one large ones (attacking the next town, marrying the king's daughter, overthrowing the chief). Willpower can be forged or broken by an ordeal (awakenings, `03`). Reason: the player must be surprised by the scale of what a body did, never by its incoherence; and the same notion keeps setting the cost of thought.

### D13 — A single type of inhabitant; the soul is a pilot
- **Reason**: reincarnation must feel natural.
- **Consequences**: no code difference between player and NPC at the body level. A body is piloted either by the AI or by a soul.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — who has a brain, and what a body's mind does under a pilot and without one** (the project lead's decisions of 12 November; the vivarium plan's second check). Still one kind of body and one mind for all. **Who has a running brain is stated per body**, so the brain written for the vivarium (D41) does not set the game's bodies acting before the living tribe (milestone 3) switches it on. Under a pilot, **three voices** decide for the body, in this order: the **soul's orders**; the **plan** the soul left, which fades with time, and faster in a body of strong Willpower; the **body's own mind**, made of its habits, the character the soul has forged and its Willpower. Reflexes (fleeing a wolf) always act. A piloted body left without an order for about a minute carries on what it was doing, or chooses for itself if it has nothing to do; a body whose soul is offline follows the plan, then its own life, and dies of it if the soul never returns. Without a pilot, the mind alone decides. Reason: a single rule covers being idle, offline, asleep and gone, and an absent player's body stays coherent with what the soul made of it.

### D14 — Souls live above the worlds
- **Reason**: soul migrations, multiple worlds, the possible end of a world.
- **Consequences**: soul data is stored in a separate service, independent of any world.

### D15 — All content as data
- **Reason**: technologies without an imposed tree, invention by assembly, evolution without recoding.
- **Consequences**: materials with properties, parts, processes, composite objects whose qualities are **computed**. Never a recipe hard-coded in the code.
- **Status** (5 November 2026, milestone 2): first content files, `content/materials.ron` (family, mass, hardness, flammability, food value, provisional colour) and `content/spawns.ron` (what lies on which terrain, in pieces per thousand tiles). The valley is scattered from them when a world is created; items are world state, in the snapshot (format v4). Parts, processes and computed qualities come with crafting.
- **Amended on 10 November 2026 — the ground's items made per chunk.** What the ground itself drops (dead wood under the trees, pebbles by the water: the only spawn rules left, `content/spawns.ron`) is made per chunk from the seed and the rules, each tile and rule rolling its own hashed chance, and never stored (`sim::items`, D31). An item has a fixed id from its tile and rule (below 2^53, so a client reading JSON numbers as floats keeps it exact). Only what a body touches becomes a stored item; the natural ones taken are remembered, so they do not come back. Nothing ready-made lies about any more: berries, fibres and flint will come from the bushes, gathered plants and the rock (the project lead: players gather raw matter and learn how to make things). The 3072 valley's snapshot fell from 18.4 MB (216,186 stored items) to under a kilobyte. Snapshot format v8: worlds made before keep their scattered items, all stored, and make no natural ones. (10 November 2026) The server makes them again each time a chunk is sent, too, and holds only the stored items and the set of the natural ones taken: before, it kept every natural item of every chunk ever sent, a memory growing with the land visited (D39).
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — content as concepts, processes and material properties** (the vivarium plan and its two checks). **Knowledge is content**: concepts and processes with their prerequisites, readable and searchable by the rules, never written in the code; a goal has **at least two paths** (a sewn hide pouch or a knotted net; drying in the sun or smoking). Where a process has **variants, the maker chooses** which, and the result follows from the materials' properties. All content shares **one energy scale** (food, fuel, work, warmth), so eating, burning and labour compare without conversion. **Long durations are written in seasons** (growth, keeping through winter, regrowth), so they follow the year when it is tuned (D09). A design page for processes is written and validated before their full model (vivarium V3). Reason: discovery and transmission (`03`) need knowledge the rules can read and search; one scale and seasonal durations keep the content true when the calendar moves.

### D16 — Object states computed on demand
- **Reason**: spoilage, wear, preservation for millions of objects.
- **Consequences**: the state is computed at the moment it is consulted, from the date and the history (smoking, cold…), not updated continuously.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — what depends on the past is written, not recomputed** (the vivarium plan's second check). A state computed from a date stays the rule, but it can only be computed from what is known: an **event whose effect depends on what happened before** (meat smoked, then the fire went out; a store losing to rodents and damp) is **written into the item's state** when it happens, and the computation starts again from there. An item carries its creation date and its state (D35). Reason: a state recomputed from the date alone would forget the fire that went out.

### D17 — Permanent event journal
- **Reason**: the world's memory, historical merit, investigations, karma, NPC memories.
- **Consequences**: significant events kept forever; fine details kept temporarily. Volume to be monitored.
- **Amended on 10 November 2026 — no world journal.** The journal held every step and every gauge, about 400 MB a real day with 100 players; nothing ever read it back, and a resumed world already dropped what came after its last snapshot. It is gone (schema v2). The world's memory is its current state; a soul's deeds will be kept in the souls' own store (D14) only until they unlock a title, a power or a skill, then folded into the soul's state (running totals such as karma kept as totals) and erased: history is a means to unlock, not an archive. Witnesses' memories (D18) and history (lazily generated where players arrive) are state too.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — a summary of each life, kept for the book of the soul** (the project lead's decisions of 12 November). The soul's deeds are still folded into its state and erased once they unlock what they unlock; but **each life leaves a summary** in the souls' store (D14), **sized by its deeds** (a short, quiet life in a few lines, a king's in more), with its body, its places and its people. The **book of the soul** reads it while the body sleeps, together with what the world still tells of those lives. The vivarium keeps its own chronicle (who did what, and why) in its report, not in a world's store (D41). Reason: the book is part of play; an archive of every act is not.

### D18 — Witnesses and perception
- **Reason**: crimes, investigations, rumors, suspicion, justice.
- **Consequences**: every action has potential witnesses; every witness keeps a memory of it. No omniscient information.

### D19 — Generic group system
- **Reason**: all of society (tribe, guild, religion, empire) rests on it.
- **Consequences**: members, roles, powers, rules, succession, hierarchy, relations, visibility. The tribe of the first version is already a group. Role powers are **social recognitions**, not locks.

### D20 — Interactions through intentions
- **Reason**: to allow generated speech and free conversation later without rebuilding anything.
- **Consequences**: menus, gestures or free sentences all translate into intentions handled the same way by the NPC's brain.

### D21 — Versioned data
- **Reason**: to evolve the world and migrate souls without breaking everything.
- **Consequences**: every save format is versioned and migratable from the start.

### D22 — Evolving security and balance
- **Reason**: like an ecosystem, balance is built through iterations.
- **Consequences**: no large initial anti-cheat system beyond server authority; we observe, we adjust.

---

*Technical skeleton decisions, approved on 5 November 2026.*

### D23 — A single repository (monorepo)
- **Reason**: client, server, content and documents evolve together; one version, one history, one CI.
- **Consequences**: structure `docs/` (this corpus), `server/` (Rust), `client/` (Godot), `content/` (world data).

### D24 — The simulation is a pure library, with no network and no clock
- **Reason**: it is the most important piece (NPCs, Willpower, needs) and the hardest to get right. Isolated, it is tested in milliseconds, runs in accelerated mode without a client (D09) and depends on no network technology.
- **Consequences**: three Rust crates from the start. `sim`: the world, the NPCs, the rules; no input/output; receives intentions and a number of ticks, returns events. `protocol`: the client ↔ server message types, in a single place. `server`: network, persistence, time loop; the only crate that talks to the outside world. **Fixed tick** (order of magnitude: 10 ticks per second of game time); acceleration only changes the tick rate, never the rules. Same inputs, same result: essential for tests and for replaying a bug.

- **Amended on 9 November 2026 — world generation is integer only**: a seed must give the same world on every machine and every build, since a world is regenerated, not stored, and keeps its generator for life. So generation uses integers only, its building blocks (the hash, the noises, the random streams) live in one module, `sim::gen`, with golden values pinned by tests, and floats are linted across the `sim` crate (`clippy::float_arithmetic` and a list of float functions in `server/sim/clippy.toml`, errors under `-D warnings`). The floats still there carry a commented exception: those in generation go when the generator is rewritten (the world by chunk); those that move bodies or tell the time are not generation and stay.

- **Amended on 10 November 2026 — one scheme to place things on the ground** (the lightness audit's placement report, `sim::gen::scatter`): everything placed on the ground is decided by a cell or a tile in **absolute coordinates**, from a hash of the seed, a **purpose** and that cell, never from a stream, never by looking at what was placed before, never for the whole map. So any chunk, alone and in any order, places the same things, and the cost follows the chunks asked for. Three building blocks: a **hashed chance per tile** (salt and pepper: the natural items), a **jittered grid** (one thing at most a cell, nudged off its centre: spacing with no check between things; the trees), and a **nested cell** (a coarse cell deciding for its fine ones: an opening in the woods; later a clump, a scree). Every random choice names its purpose in one table, all different and pinned by tests, so a new layer never moves an old one; the older salts keep their values. Next on the same blocks: the flora's patches, then rocks, dens and the surface signs of deposits (the latter hashed with the resource key, never the seed).
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — a generator version with each world, and two seeds for the vivarium** (the vivarium plan's second check). Each world **stores the version of the generator** that made it: adding rock shelters or flint to the generator must not change the valley of a world already made, which keeps its version for life. The vivarium (D41) separates a **map seed** from a **run seed**: the same land lived many times, the same run replayed exactly. Reason: a world is regenerated, not stored (amendment of 9 November), and a judgement needs runs that differ only in what is being judged.
  - **Built on 13 November 2026 — versions 1 and 2** (the rocks' page, `content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/roches/PAGE.md`, step 1; the project lead's yes of the same day). The version is a field of the valley's spec, `generator`, stored with the world (snapshot format v10). A world saved before it reads as **version 1**, the valley as it was, and keeps it: a test pins every chunk frame of a version-1 valley, byte for byte, to what the server sent before. **New worlds are made by version 2**, the game's too: the game makes its world anew at each start, so nothing needs protecting there. Version 2 keeps version 1's terrain, waters, woods, trees and items tile for tile, and adds **the rock under the ground** (D42).
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — a world's seed is its secret** (the security audit of 20 November 2026, F1; the project lead's call Q3: a secret random seed per real world, seed 1 the public development seed). Seed 1 made every valley, so the whole world, every tile, river, tree, plant and natural item and the rock under them, could be read offline from the public code before playing, and the ores to come would have followed. A new world is now cut from a seed drawn at its birth (`server::secret`: the standard library's hasher keyed from the system's entropy, no crate added), kept for life in its snapshot (`MapSpec`, no schema change), never printed and never sent. `--seed <N>` asks for a seed; `--dev` without one keeps seed 1, since a server that hands every client the map's overview keeps no secret; the test worlds keep theirs. The generator stays integer-only and deterministic: the secret is the seed, never the code.

### D25 — Transport: WebSocket
- **Reason**: native in Godot 4 (`WebSocketPeer`), mature in Rust (`tokio-tungstenite`). Goes through everywhere (firewalls, proxies), trivial TLS later, opens the door to a web client and to administration tools in a browser. The game is slow: no need for UDP.
- **Consequences**: no high-level Godot multiplayer API. The client has a single connection, a single network module.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026 — text and binary frames** (lightness plan D3, protocol v17): every message is a text frame of JSON but the chunk, which travels in a **binary frame** (D26). The client tells them apart by the frame's kind (`was_string_packet()`), in its network module only.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, in the evening** (lightness plan D4, protocol v18): `hello` may grow to 64 KiB, the chunks a reconnecting client holds in it (D08); every other message is still held to 4 KiB.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, late in the evening** (lightness plan D5, protocol v19): the transport does not change; what travels on each connection now follows its place (D08): the bodies within 150 m, the events of the chunks it holds.
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — the door** (the security audit of 20 November 2026, findings N1, N2, N4, N5, N6; the project lead's decisions Q1 and Q2 of that day). The reason: any page open in a player's browser could connect to the server on 127.0.0.1 and, through it, read what his client sees, pilot his body, crash the debug server with one message and write into his terminal. The transport does not change; what the door checks does. **`Origin`**: an upgrade carrying an `Origin` header is a browser page, never the Godot desktop client (it sends none), and is refused with 403 (the lead's decision Q2: refuse every `Origin` for now; a web client would need an allowlist in the same place). **Liveness**: the server pings every connection at an interval (`--ping-every`, 20 s) and drops one from which nothing came, pongs included, for `--drop-silent-after` (60 s, three pings missed): a player who leaves the game open stays, since the transport answers pings for him (Godot's does); a peer that vanished, or a silent socket taking a place, does not. **Places per address**: `--connections-per-address` (8: a household, or a few clients on a development machine) beside `--max-connections`. The numbers are the server's flags with those defaults, not decisions of the lead. **The log**: client-derived text in a log line is escaped and bounded (`net::loggable`), a connection's bad messages write ten lines then are counted silently, and a hundred of them close the connection (`COMPLAINTS_LOGGED`, `COMPLAINTS_MAX`). **Off-map targets**: the simulation refuses a `move_to`, `drink` or `dev_teleport` target off the map with the existing refusals, before any arithmetic on it (D03: the client's coordinates are not trusted), and the arithmetic on a target is saturating or unsigned, so the debug build, overflow checks on, no longer panics. **`--dev` stays as it is** (the lead's decision Q1): the dev tools are not gated and need no secret.
- **Amended on 20 November 2026, later — the door's leftovers** (the security audit of 20 November 2026, lot F: findings N3, N7, N8, N9, N10; the lead's decision Q1 kept: `--dev` is not gated). The transport and the messages do not change; four more things the door bounds, and one it says. **Sessions per address**: an address opens at most `--sessions-per-address` connections a minute (60); the next are closed before the upgrade, at the cost of an accept, and the address's refusals are logged once a minute; a session writes one line when its hello is answered and one when it ends, its error folded in (N8: a connect-and-leave loop cost a core and three lines a session). **`chunk_missing` once**: it is honoured only in answer to a `chunk_same` or a `chunk_fruit`, once; asked for a chunk whose frame itself left, it is a bad message (counted with the others) and nothing is built (N7: one client made the server send seventeen times the bytes it received). **A teleport a second**: under `--dev` a connection teleports at most once every `--teleport-every` (1 s; 0 for the tests that hop); a teleport asked sooner is dropped. Measured on the 3072 valley: one client teleporting once a tick, the simulation's own cap, kept the eight chunk threads at 6.8 processors (a view of 361 chunks built cold at each teleport) and the simulation thread at 0.23, the trunks of the chunks around the target; four clients, 6.9 and 0.63. The cost is the pool, not the simulation thread; one teleport a second a connection divides both by ten (N3). **The pool's lanes are bounded**: a connection's lane holds at most `pool::LANE_JOBS_MOST` (512) jobs waiting, a view and a part of the next; past that a chunk's job is refused and asked again as the lane's jobs are done, so a view moved at every tick piles up nothing (N9). **Unknown fields are told**: a client message's unknown fields are still ignored (the protocol stays forward compatible: a newer client may add some), and are told in the log once a connection (`protocol::unknown_fields`, N10). The numbers are the server's flags with those defaults, not decisions of the lead.

### D26 — Message encoding: JSON, behind a single door
- **Reason**: readable in logs, parsed natively by Godot, trivial to debug. The volumes of the first version are small.
- **Consequences**: encoding is confined to a single module on each side, so we can switch to binary the day it matters without touching the rest. Every message carries a type and a protocol version. The Rust types in `protocol` are the reference; the client mirrors them. **Shape of the dialogue** (D05, D08, D20): client → server, intentions only; server → client, an initial state of what the body perceives, then changes. The client never receives the whole world: the ground travels in chunks around the body (D08, D31).
- **Amended on 11 November 2026 — the chunk in binary, behind the same door** (lightness plan D3, protocol v17): the day came for the one message that weighs. A chunk was ~16.6 KB of JSON (a word a tile, a species name a tree, materials spelled out) and the client spent ~1.2 s of a login reading them; it is now a compact **binary frame** of ~2 KB: a 16-byte header (the chunk, its **version** from D2, flags, its side), **a byte a tile** in the client's terrain codes, the currents only for its water tiles, then trees and plants as an offset in millimetres within the chunk and an **index** into the kinds `world_state` lists, natural items as a slot and a material index (their id and tile follow), stored items as id, tile and material index. Every other message stays JSON, readable; the chunk is printed decoded on demand (`--dump-chunk X,Y`). The door is unchanged: `protocol` alone writes and reads the layout (`encode_chunk`, `decode_chunk`, the layout in `ChunkFrame`'s doc), `client/net/protocol.gd` alone decodes it into packed arrays the world view reads as they are. **The bodies leave the chunk**: a chunk describes the place, which changes rarely and can be kept and shared; the bodies standing in it follow its frame in a small JSON message (`chunk_bodies`). So the server **encodes a chunk once** per version of its ground and of its items, and sends the same bytes to every client asking (a cache bounded like the plants'). A golden frame written by the server's tests and decoded by the client's (`client/tests/fixtures/chunk_v17.bin`) keeps both sides on one layout. Not now: compression (a server dependency, and Godot's WebSocket has no per-message deflate) and run-length tiles, ~1 KB more each, if the bandwidth ever asks.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, in the evening — what the client holds is not sent again** (lightness plan D4, protocol v18): `chunk_same`, `chunk_missing`, `view` (replacing `chunk_hidden`), `world_state`'s `world` and `hello`'s `held`, described in D08. The frame's header version is the chunk's one version (D2, made exact: every change to what the frame carries bumps it, items dropped included), and the server's frame cache is keyed by it; the same bytes a version, so a version is enough for both sides to know a frame. The layout of the frame does not change.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, late in the evening — interest by place** (lightness plan D5 and A6, protocol v19): no new message; the meaning of three changes (D08). `chunk_bodies` follows only the frames within the creatures' range (150 m), and comes too for a chunk held that comes within it; `body_left` is sent when a body walks out of that range or the range moves away from it; `body_walks` is sent again each time a walking body walks into another chunk (never at each tile), and the client walks it along its route in between, by time: `body_walks` and `chunk_bodies`' bodies gain `arrivals`, the tick each waypoint of the route is reached, the route holding the points where the pace changes.
- **Amended on 14 November 2026 — the chunk frame may carry a height a tile** (D43, protocol v25): a flag of the frame's header (bit 1) says the chunk's heights follow its currents, one `i16` a tile in decimetres. A frame with the flag down is byte for byte what it was.
- **Amended on 19 November 2026 — compact frames, the far view in binary, room to grow** (step 4, network thinker's ideas 2b and 3; protocol v30). A binary message now starts with its **kind** (`BINARY_CHUNK` 0, `BINARY_FAR_VIEW` 1), and a chunk frame's header is kind, flags, side, x, y, version, followed by the length of its ground. Its **tiles go as a palette and runs** (the terrains it holds, then `(index, length varint)` runs; one terrain alone, no runs): 1,024 bytes → 55 on average. The **far view** is no longer JSON but a binary message of `(letter, length varint)` runs over the same cells: 65,614 → 10,465 bytes. **Room is kept, at no cost, for what is coming**: a tree's and a plant's kind goes through a palette of `u16` kinds per chunk (a planet's species, past 255, fit; a palette index past it is an unknown kind, as an index past `world_state`'s lists already was), and the high four bits of its fruit byte are its **growth stage**, 0 for every tree and plant today and not yet read by the client. Nothing else changes: the same content (every frame of the 768 valley of the first generator, decoded and written again in v29's layout, gives v29's bytes exactly; the client decodes the 361 frames and the far view of a login to exactly what it decoded from v29's). A login's frames **880,546 → 543,312 bytes (−38 %)**, its far view −84 %; the client's decoding of them 24–25 → 27–32 ms headless (alternated, 21 runs each), the far view's 0.5 → 1.5–1.7 ms. The golden messages both sides' tests read are now `client/tests/fixtures/chunk_v30.bin` and `far_v30.bin`. The world's identity changes once with the frames' bytes, so clients drop the frames they held and load them anew. Heights as deltas and deflate were left out (no world but the test worlds has heights; deflate would be a dependency for −26 % more).
- **Amended on 20 November 2026, later — the window past the eyes** (the second security audit of 20 November 2026, lot G: findings 4.C1, 1.A3, 2.F1, 2.F2 and 1.A2 of its reports; the project lead's decisions of that day: the dawn refresh accepted as a daily, chunk-coarse tell, the range judged in metres). No message changes. **`chunk_same` past the eyes**: a chunk out of the creatures' range is answered `chunk_same` at the version the client holds (`hello`'s `held`, or the server's mirror of its cache when the chunk comes back into the view), whatever moved in it: the version was an oracle, 360 `chunk_same` and one fresh frame told a reconnecting client where a hidden body had picked something up 200 m away, though no event had (F6). The frame goes when the chunk comes within range, and at dawn (`refresh`: the daily tell). A `chunk_same` still waiting for its `chunk_missing` keeps waiting across a dawn (F1). **The range in metres**: the creatures' range holds every chunk a tile of which may lie within 150 m of the body (its nearest tile, not its centre: five chunks along the axes, fewer on the diagonals), and what men do to the ground is told within 150 m in metres, as `World::sight` judges an act on it, whatever the chunk: an item change 147 m away is told, one 151 m away is not; the bodies stay told by chunk, as since D18's stand-in. **The door**: an address's bad-message lines are budgeted by the minute over all its connections (thirty, three connections' worth), counted and not written past it (A2: a connect, complain and leave loop got ten lines a session). A chunk job that panics still answers, so no deferred chunk waits for ever (F2).

### D27 — Content in RON, read by the server only
- **Reason**: content (D15) is made of nested structures and categories (materials, parts, processes). RON is Rust's native data format: it expresses them directly, accepts comments and is checked at load time. The client does not need to read it: it receives the names and visual identifiers from the server at connection.
- **Consequences**: hidden rules stay on the server effortlessly (D05). Content is validated at server startup: an inconsistent file prevents launch; a CI test loads all the content at every commit.
- **Status** (5 November 2026, milestone 2): built. `sim::content` parses and validates (unique `snake_case` ids, resolved references, sane values); `server::content` is the only place files are read, from `--content <DIR>` or the first of `content`, `../content`, `../../content`. The server prints what it loaded at startup.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — the vivarium reads content too, as a base plus overrides** (the vivarium plan's second check). The vivarium (D41) is a second reader of the same content, through the same validation. What it changes for a test (a named compression factor, a smaller herd) lives in **override files laid over the base content**, never in a second set that would drift apart. It judges with the private content (D30) where it is present, with the public placeholders otherwise. Reason: what is tuned in the vivarium must be what the game plays. **Status (20 November 2026, audit F8)**: not built yet. No override file and no private lookup exist (`server/vivarium/src`, `server/sim/src/content.rs`): the vivarium reads the base files and the compression factors are edited in them. The decision stands; the code is to catch up with it in the sim phase.
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — ceilings on the content's numbers** (security audit of 20 November 2026, S4). The files refused a zero and a wrong percent, but took any rate, mass or budget up to `u32::MAX`: a thirst of four billion points a day overflowed the gauges' arithmetic on a body's first tick (a panic on the debug build, garbage on the release one), a path budget of four billion let one search run a whole tick. Every such field now has a **ceiling checked at load** (`Content::validate_bounds`), the refusal naming the file and the field ("needs.ron: thirst_per_day is at most 1000000"): a million for the gauges' rates, the kilojoules, the grams and the geology's metres; ten thousand for weights and the percents not already bounded; a thousand for tiles, years and metres of height or reach. Each is ten to a thousand times today's placeholder values (D30), which all pass, and far below what overflows.

### D28 — Persistence: event journal + snapshots, SQLite first
- **Reason**: the simulation lives in memory; we persist the event journal (D17) and periodic snapshots. SQLite is a single file, with no server to install, sufficient for a tribe and a valley.
- **Consequences**: storage goes through a single interface (`Storage`); replacing it with PostgreSQL only touches that module. Schema and snapshots versioned with migrations from day one (D21). Soul data lives in a separate file, behind its own interface (D14).
- **Status** (milestone 1 audit, 5 November 2026): schema v1, snapshot format v2 (calendar, tick, map, bodies), one snapshot per game day plus the journal. Nothing is pruned yet; the retention policy is an open question (`06`).
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — one server per world file** (the security audit of 20 November 2026, verification of audit 3): two servers started on the same `--data` file both ran, each resuming from the other's snapshot and braiding two worlds into one database. A server now holds a sidecar `<data>.lock` (`std::fs::File::try_lock`, no dependency, released by the kernel when the process ends however it ends) for its whole run, and a second server on the same file is refused at start. No schema change; ephemeral worlds hold no lock.
- **Amended on 10 November 2026 — first storage diet** (the lightness audit, `content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/lightness-audit/`): the world journal is removed (D17): it held every step and gauge, about 400 MB a real day with 100 players, and nothing read it back. Only the **last three snapshots** are kept (each was a full copy of the world, 18 MB a game day, never pruned). A snapshot writes the map as its spec straight from the map in place, no longer copying every tile first (57 MB on the 3072 valley): 13 ms instead of 22 ms per snapshot on that valley, the same bytes. Losing up to a few minutes of play on a crash is accepted by the project lead (five minutes). So a game with players now **saves every five real minutes** instead of each game day (two real hours), and **on a thread of its own**: the simulation hands over a copy of the world and never waits for the disk. The copy is cheap because the world's map is shared (`Arc<Map>`), never copied; the network shares the same map instead of holding a second copy of its tiles (57 MB on the 3072 valley). A headless run, which plays days in seconds, still saves at each game day. Next, the target design: current state only, as rows, souls and their deeds in their own file (D14), deeds kept only until they unlock a title, power or skill.
- **Amended on 11 November 2026 — what men change, stored chunk by chunk** (lightness plan, D2; the project lead's answers of the same day, plan §8). Every tree is identified **by its placement cell** for the whole life of the world, with no table of ids: its chunk, the grid it was placed on (the big trees' wide grid, the narrow grid between them, the meadows' grid) and its cell on that grid counted from the chunk's first cell, plus its rank in a meadow group (`sim::trees::TreeKey`, 13 bits). The same tree has the same key every time its chunk is placed, in any order; two trees of a chunk never share one. What men change in a chunk is its **`chunk_changes`** (`sim::changes::ChunkChanges`) with a **version per chunk** (how many times men changed it, 0 and no row while it is as the seed made it): today the natural items taken off the ground (moved out of the snapshot), and, ready but not yet made by the simulation, the trees felled (their keys), the trees planted (key, species id, stage, day) and constructions (kind, tile, day). They are stored **beside the snapshot, a row a chunk** (**schema v3**: table `chunk_changes`, `x`, `y`, `version`, `format_version`, `tick` of the checkpoint that wrote it, `data`), and a checkpoint writes **only the chunks whose version moved** since the last one; the snapshot (format **v9**) no longer holds them. A chunk's data (format v1) is a few lists of varints: about a byte a felled tree, so a dense chunk felled whole (40 to 60 trees) is 45 to 70 bytes, about 100 bytes on disk with the row; a dense forest felled over a square kilometre (about 980 chunks) about 50 KB of data, 100 KB on disk, where a table of tree ids would cost 0.5 to 1 MB. Migration: a v2 database gains the table; its v8 snapshot's taken items become their chunks' changes when it is read (a version an item), and the next checkpoint writes them in their rows. The rows hold the latest state only: the two older snapshots kept as a margin are read with the latest changes. The chunk's version is what clients will compare (D4); the protocol does not change yet. (Since D4, the same evening, it also counts items dropped and lifted, and clients compare it: D08.)
- **Amended on 11 November 2026, late evening — a crash starts a new epoch** (follow-up to lightness plan D4, the project lead's call of the same day). A crash loses what men changed since the last checkpoint, so a chunk's version can go back to a number a client still holds with other content, and the client would keep a stale frame (D08). The server now writes a **clean stop mark** after the final snapshot of a proper stop (Ctrl-C or `SIGTERM`, a day limit, the end of a headless run), and reads and clears it at startup. When the mark is missing (the last run crashed, was killed, or failed to write), the world's **epoch** goes up by one and is folded into the world's identity (`world_state`'s `world`): clients drop every frame they hold and reload. After a clean stop the epoch, and so the identity, is unchanged and nothing is resent. The world's **birth**, a random 64-bit number drawn when its database is made (SQLite's `random()`), is folded in too, so a world made anew on the same seed (`--reset`) never shares an identity with the old one. **Schema v4**: table `run_state`, one row (`birth`, `epoch`, `clean`); a database that already held a world gets its birth and counts as crashed once, a new one starts at epoch 0, clean. The startup write is synchronous (`synchronous = FULL`), so a power cut cannot bring back a mark already cleared. Ephemeral worlds (`--ephemeral`) and `--dump-chunk` neither read nor clear the mark and have neither birth nor epoch: their identity is as before, so two ephemeral servers on the same seed stay the same world to a client (a test reconnects across two of them). `SIGTERM`, how scripts stop a server, now stops it cleanly like Ctrl-C; `SIGKILL` and a crash do not.
- **Amended on 19 November 2026 — the continent born once and kept** (step 4 of the lightening, thinker report `reflexion-2026-11-19/2-monde.md`, I1). A world cut from the atlas (`--world plan:<SEED>`, `--world vivarium`) was cut at every start from its continent born again: 1,024 cells a side for seed 14, 6 to 13 s and 140 MB at its peak, every time the same. The server now keeps each continent once born in **a SQLite file of its own** (`continents.sqlite` by default, `--continents <PATH>`; schema v1, a row a seed and side: `Continent::to_bytes` with its own layout version and checksum, and `Continent::fingerprint`, checked on reading), and a later start reads it back and cuts the window from it with no birth. In a file of its own, not in the world's database, because it is no world's state: the same for every world of a seed, kept across `--reset`, and read by the test worlds, which are never saved (the vivarium's is one). The simulation stays pure (D24): it turns a continent into bytes and back, and `plan::born_within` runs a world's making with the continents given, telling which it had to bear; the server reads and writes. A damaged row is left out and the continent born again and kept in its place. Measured (the vivarium, release, three alternated starts, medians): start 10.4 → 5.6 s, resident at listening 90 → 30 MB, peak 137 → 121 MB; the first start of a seed keeps it (about 60 MB on disk, a peak of 265 MB once while it is written). The world's bytes are the same as born (a test: the vivarium made on its kept continent, tile for tile). Not done: the plan stored compact (I7); a world's other birth outputs.
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — the world's identity is its birth, not its map** (the security audit of 20 November 2026, K1). `world_state`'s `world` was a hash of the map's spec, the seed in it, and of a few chunks as the seed made them: with a secret seed, no hash of it may go on the wire. The identity is now the hash of the content's kinds (what the frames name by index) folded with the world's birth and epoch, nothing of the land. An ephemeral world, which has no stored birth, draws one at start: every start of an ephemeral server is another world to a client, which reloads its view (the reconnection test now returns to one server and checks that a second one is another world). A stored world keeps its identity across clean stops, as before.
- **Amended on 20 November 2026 — the saves hardened** (security audit of 20 November 2026, lot C: findings S1 to S4, S7 to S9; the project lead's decisions Q6 and Q7 of the same day). (1) **A damaged latest snapshot falls back to the one before it** (Q6): the three kept snapshots were a margin nothing read; now the server reads the newest it can, drops the damaged rows (a row of a format no build wrote, or of unreadable or crafted data), says so, and **counts the run as a crash** (the epoch goes up: what came after the older snapshot is lost to clients as a crash loses it). A snapshot of a format **newer** than the build is not damage: it is refused as before, so an older build never buries what a newer one wrote. (2) **A crafted or damaged save is refused, never panicked on**: a calendar with a zero length, a map past 16,384 tiles a side or of a generator the build does not know, a tick or an epoch past the database's integers, a snapshot whose columns are not what the schema says. (3) **A panic in the storage thread stops the server** as a failed save does ("persistence failed, stopping"), and the stop reports it; before, the world played on with nothing saved until a stop that panicked again. (4) **The storage queue holds one snapshot** beside the one being written: when the writer is behind (a headless run saving a day a tick, a disk that stalls), the checkpoint is skipped, counted and logged (at 1, 2, 4... skips, and the total at the stop), never piled up in memory; a paced game tries again at the next tick, and the final snapshot of a stop always waits its turn. (5) **The first checkpoint is taken at the first birth** of the run, not five minutes after the start: a crash in the first minutes no longer loses a new world whole. (6) **The world's and the continents' files are made for their owner alone** (mode 0600; SQLite made them 0644, and gives the WAL and shared-memory files the main file's mode); a file the server finds is left as it is. (7) **A SQLite file that is not a save is refused**, not adopted: one with tables of its own and no schema version, or a version without the save's table (no marker added to the schema: the tables are the marker). A full disk keeps the clean stop as before (Q7). No schema, snapshot format or protocol change: the same bytes, read and refused with more care. (8, **20 November 2026, later — lot G, 2.C1**) A fallback keeps the land as the lost run left it (the `chunk_changes` rows are one a chunk, the newest checkpoint's wins) and **says how many chunks** a checkpoint newer than the resumed snapshot had changed, in the same note: the lead's call, said rather than undone (no schema change).

### D29 — One server, one simulation thread
- **Reason**: the simulation is deterministic and sequential, the network is asynchronous; mixing them creates the hardest bugs there are.
- **Consequences**: the simulation loop runs on a single thread and receives intentions through a queue; the network (tokio) runs alongside and only translates. The split into regions (D07) will come later with several loops of this kind.
- **Precision** (milestone 1 audit, 5 November 2026): the queue holds **one pending intention per body**, the latest wins, taken before each tick. A client flooding `move_to` costs one path search per tick and nothing to anyone else. Refusals go back to the pilot alone; moves are published to all. The listener also bounds what the outside can cost: a handshake deadline, a maximum message size, a maximum number of connections (`--max-connections`).
- **Amended on 10 November 2026**: a second thread of the server's own, **the storage thread**, writes the snapshots; the simulation thread only hands it a copy of the world, and never waits for the disk (D28).
