A world cut from the atlas (D41) gives a whole 2 km cell one biome, and the valley's woods grew only where it was a forest: its grassland, 39 % of the zone, was bare, with no habitat at all, so neither a tree nor a plant. Now every dry tile outside a forest biome is a **meadow** (`Habitat::Meadow`), so the meadow's plants grow there and the valley's groups of trees come by the woods and the water as they do in the valley (so a forest's edge is never a wall: groups within 40 m of it, thinning out); and each open biome has **its own trees, as content** (`content/open_ground.ron`, read like `geology.ron`, D27): trees standing alone and groves a square kilometre, and a grove's size, drawn per cell of the meadows' grid (one group at most every 24 m) from the seed and the cell, thinned by the dense and light regions as the forest is; the kinds are the lone trees' of the region's stands. Placeholders: grassland 200 lone trees and 50 groves of 3 to 8 a square kilometre (about 5.8 trees a hectare on the zone, 168 groves and 336 lone trees on its 181 ha of grassland), wooded steppe 150 and 120 groves of 4 to 8, steppe 30 and 10, marsh 100 and 30; desert, tundra and alpine none. The forest itself is unchanged, and the valley (versions 1 and 2) has no biome and keeps its trees tile for tile. Bushes are the flora's, by habitat (`flora.ron`): nothing of theirs is set per biome yet.
  - **Keyed on the continent, 15 November 2026** (found by the safety net's sheets, D39: every 2 km window of the continent held the same woods, the same stands, clearings and groves, because the woods' noises and the round patches' cells were keyed on the window's own tiles, not on its place on the continent; the vivarium, one window, could not show it). Every layer drawn from the seed and a position is now keyed on the **world's position** (`Map::origin`, the window's corner on the continent; `gen::scatter::Frame`): the woods' noises, wetness and round patches, the trees' grids, stands and gaps, the plants, the rocks, the hills and the ragged borders of the atlas's windows. Two windows differ; the same ground drawn through two overlapping windows holds the same trees (a test on each); the valley, its own world at the origin, is byte-identical (terrain, woods, trees, plants and rocks hashed before and after). The vivarium's trees moved; its camps and fords did not. A round patch whose centre lies off a window still holds nothing on it, so a window's first tiles may differ from a neighbour's: a known seam, to settle when the continent is drawn in one piece.
  - **Birch on the banks, 25 November 2026** (the biosphere audit `content/ai-reserve/review/vivarium/lot-5/audit-biosphere-2026-11-25.md`, proposal 1; the project lead: "Ok, va pour ça. Je suis partant."). The birch, a pioneer of the river gravels, grew in every habitat but the riverside, where only willow and alder stood, so flint (on the bank grass and the fords, D57) and birch hardly ever met. It now has `riverside: 50` in `content/species.ron`, half the willow's weight, so it competes for the bank's stands and forms its own; nothing else about placement changes. **Every world changes**: the trees are placed from the seed and the content with no generator version, so the banks of every existing world, the vivarium's included, get birches in place of some willows and alders when their chunks are next placed; a run before and a run after no longer compare on this point. Nothing is stored per tree but a felling (by key), and no bark is stored, so no saved state disagrees. Measured on three valleys of 512 tiles (seeds 2, 5, 9): birches 330, 275, 135 → 525, 352, 328, of them on the banks 0 → 164, 62, 160; birches within 4 tiles of a flint 7, 2, 2 → 16, 4, 4: the banks' wood starts two tiles off the water on forest ground, where no flint lies, so the meeting stays rare. The rock, the soils and a flint that reads the rock wait for the generator round.
  - Every number here is a dial of the generator, to be tuned by looking at maps; the mechanism is the decision.

### D38 — Development tools only behind `--dev`
- **Reason**: the valley is 3 km across and meant to grow far more; looking at a place far away (a river's mouth, a brook, a wood) should not take half an hour of walking. But any shortcut a client can ask for is a cheat the day other people play, so it must not exist in play at all (D05: the server decides).
- **Consequences** (8 November 2026): the server takes `--dev`. Only then does it send, after `world_state`, a `dev_overview` (the whole map, coarse: one letter a cell of at least 8 tiles, 512 cells across at most whatever the map's size), and accept `dev_teleport` (protocol v12), which the simulation plays as an intention (`Intention::Teleport`: the walkable tile nearest the target, within 64 tiles, at once). Without `--dev` there is no overview and a teleport is ignored. On the client, M opens the overview turned as the game shows the map, a click teleports; Ctrl+click on the ground does the same nearby. Further development tools go behind the same flag.

### D39 — Performance by design: measured, budgeted, never at the cost of what is seen
- **Reason**: the world grows richer (trees, then 39 species of flora, D37 and `content/ai-reserve/notes/10-flora.md`) and larger (3 km across, meant for ten times and more). Optimisations bolted on afterwards are costly and risky; laid down early, most cost little and change nothing on screen. The project lead's rule: lighter, but never at the cost of the look or the game. Examined on 9 November 2026: the wind in the trees is computed by the graphics card (a few operations a pixel, no redraw on the processor) and is kept; F4 lets the project lead measure it.
- **Consequences** (9 November 2026):
  - **Measure first**: in `--dev` (D38), F3 shows what a frame costs (frames a second, the longest frame, script time, draw calls, canvas items, nodes, memory, chunks and trees held, chunks waiting to be built); F4 turns the wind off and on, to measure it. A fixed scene benchmarked at every pull request comes next, so a regression shows at once.
  - **The shipped game's size, measured** (11 November 2026): a Linux export preset (`client/export_presets.cfg`: every resource but `tests/`, `tools/` and `.dev/`; the `dev/` scripts ship, the play code calls them and the server gates them behind `--dev`, D38) and `client/tools/export_size.sh`, which exports headless, never a window, and prints the pack's weight, what weighs in it by source folder, the heaviest files, and the whole game once the export templates of the Godot in use are installed. First measure: the pack **86.6 MB**, all of it the imported textures (trees 64.5, ground 14.7, flora 6.5, decorations 0.8; the heaviest file the flora atlas, 6.5 MB, then each big tree 4.6 to 5.0 MB), **49.8 MB zipped**; the engine (a Linux release template, about 70 to 90 MB) is measured once the templates are installed. The number goes in every pull request that changes what the client ships; a CI step printing it would touch `.github/` and is the project lead's call.
  - **Budget**: 60 frames a second at the worst place (a dense wood at the farthest zoom) on the project lead's machine; the world's share of a frame within about 4 ms of script time. Numbers to revise as the machine park becomes known.
  - **The techniques, in this order, each kept only if it changes nothing visible**: (1) images in **atlases** with one material, so trees and plants of every kind batch into a few draw calls even sorted by depth; (2) what is many and still drawn **in batches** (a multimesh a species and chunk), the sway staying in the shader; (3) chunks **built off the main thread** (Godot's worker threads), the main thread only showing the result; (4) a **compact protocol** for tiles and flora (a byte a tile, a species index and offset a plant: D26's "binary the day it matters" starts there); then, if the measures ask: (5) the still ground **baked**, not per chunk in the world's space (that resamples the art and costs memory, 10 November 2026) but **into screen pages at the current zoom**, at 1:1 (done on 20 November 2026, below); (6) **detail by zoom**: what would be under 2 or 3 pixels is not drawn; (7) the smallest ground flora painted by the ground shader from a density map rather than as objects.
  - **Measured on 9 November 2026** (the project lead's machine, a dense wood at the farthest zoom): about 5 fps at first; the see-through looked through every item for every tree under the mouse (fixed); then F5/F6 showed the trees' shader about 30 ms and the ground's 18 ms of a 52 ms frame; both lightened with nothing changed on screen, about 45 fps (22 ms: trees 8, ground 7, the rest 7). The trees' images are read a level sharper only up close, easing to the hardware's own level at zoom 0.5 and farther, where the eye cannot tell.
  - **Texture compression** (BPTC, best quality) was tested on the trees: four times less video memory (36 MB to 9), the contours a little softer only when enlarged. Kept in reserve, an import setting to turn on the day video memory becomes the limit, measured with F3.
  - **Amended on 9 November 2026: all art is compressed for the GPU, BC7 at best quality** (the lead's call after side-by-side comparisons, near and far: the loss is "very, very little" and the gain huge). It is the one exception to "nothing visible", and the line stays there: no lossier format (S3TC was visibly dirty), no smaller images without the lead's look. Measured by the light panel: the textures' video memory 159 MB to 43 (−73 %), their loading 1–1.7 s to 0.02 s, the packed download 46 MB to 26; on the 1:1 crops about 40 dB (37–42 by kind), no blocks, no shift of colour. Every tree has mipmaps (four had none). An image's last row holds its foot: the art tool keeps it nearly opaque so the compression's rounding never lifts a tree off the ground.
  - **Amended on 10 November 2026: one sharpness for every image, shipped at 307 px a metre** (the project lead's rules, `09-visual-production.md` section 4). The camera stops at zoom 2.5 (#176). Each image is made at the size it takes on screen at that closest zoom, its kind's shown height (D40) × 64 × 2.5 × a screen's stretch of the 1280 × 720 viewport, in two sizes. **Originals at 384 px a shown metre** (stretch 2.4: the lead's 3072 × 1920 screen, of which Godot keeps 3072 × 1728) are the archive, never shipped. **Shipped at 307.2 px a shown metre** (stretch 1.92: a 2560-px-wide screen, 1440p): perfectly sharp at zoom 2.5 on such a screen and below, magnified 1.25 times at the closest zoom only on 3072-px and 4K screens, for 36 % less weight (× 0.64) on every image. Changing the shipped size is a parameter (`SHARP_SCREEN_STRETCH` in `content/tools/flora_art.py`) and a rerun. The tool reduces the originals to the shipped size, never enlarges, and lists every source under its original target, the only ones to make again (none today); trees are made only with `--trees`, at `--tree-zoom` (2.5 by default); it used to size them by their true height, which would have made a 5,632 px oak. Shipped: a chanterelle 25 px, a dandelion 61, an elder 768, where all were 384 before zoom 4's sizes; an oak shown 8.1 m will be 2,494 px. Atlas images start on a 4-pixel block, 8 pixels apart, so no compression block or near mipmap mixes two plants; skyline packing fills the atlas at 86 %. Measured (BC7), against main with zoom 4's sizes: the flora atlas 4096 × 5992 → 4096 × 1240, video memory 32.7 → 6.8 MB, zipped download 17.1 → 3.8 MB; the decorations (no mipmaps, tufts 169 px wide, flowers 154, pebbles 86) 2.53 → 0.81 MB and 1.49 → 0.52 MB; all textures 68.1 → 40.4 MB of video memory, 41.8 → 27.6 MB downloaded. Against the sizes before the rule (384 px for every plant): flora 11.2 → 6.8 MB, decorations 2.55 → 0.81 MB, with the tall plants sharper than then.
  - **The ground's cost must not grow with the number of terrains**: deserts, rock, snow, lakes, towns will come; their textures go in an array and a pixel blends only the two or three terrains that touch it, never all of them. Designed with the first new terrain, when it can be seen. Its cost follows the screen, never the map's size.
  - **The ground made light, 10 November 2026** (lightness audit, pass 3; the lead approved the two changes of look). The client keeps every tile's terrain as a **byte** in a grid laid over the map again and again (`TerrainGrid`, 640 × 640, never growing with the map), and a chunk reads itself and its margin once, as bytes, when it builds: no strings, no callables. Each terrain's canvas draws **one quad a chunk**, and a toroidal **ground index** texture (`GroundIndex`, two bytes a tile: which canvases draw it, the forest's spill at its corner) tells the shader which pixels are its own: the per-tile meshes are gone, the picture the same to the pixel. The bank's field (`ShoreField`) blurs by **one separable convolution** of the tiles (byte-identical to the three box blurs) and keeps only the distance at 4 px a tile, the fords and the current at one pixel a tile (approved: the water's ripples shift slightly, the banks are the same). The ground shader **reads a layer only where it can show** (an exact bound; the same picture). The **ground textures keep their 1024 px and gain mipmaps**, read a level sharper than the hardware's own (the lead's option B, after 512 px proved softer up close; the maximum zoom becomes 2.5): as crisp at zoom 2.5 and 3 (the captures' sharpness equal to the hundredth), and with the camera moved half a pixel at zooms 0.4 and 1 the shimmer is 7 to 9 times lower than without mipmaps. The ground's eleven textures take 14.7 MB imported (video memory and download) instead of 11.0. Measured on the lead's machine, before → after, two alternated runs each, the machine shared with other work: loading the 19 × 19 chunks **15.6–17.7 s → 7.8–9.1 s** headless and **23.7–24.2 s → 12.4–12.8 s** in a window (an earlier, quieter round: 14–23 s → 5.3–5.9 s headless); a chunk's build median 29–35 ms → 8.5–13 ms, plus the meshes (3–4 s in all) gone; client memory 229 → 158 MB; video memory **171–173 → 97 MB** (vertex buffers 93–95 → 35, textures 78 → 62); the ground's share of the GPU frame 1.2–1.3 → 0.5–0.6 ms at zoom 1.5 and 4.3–4.7 → 0.7–0.8 ms at zoom 0.4. Next, if the measures ask: the build on a worker thread, several builds a frame behind the loading screen, the decorations from the same bytes.
  - **The trees made light, 10 November 2026** (lightness audit, pass 4; nothing changes on screen: captures before and after identical to the pixel, near and far, in a dense wood and at an edge, a body behind, in front of and beside a tree, piloted or not). A tree is no longer a node with a dictionary of its own: a chunk's trees are a **`Trees.Stand`**, packed arrays (its place, its rectangle, its image, its see-through flags) and the cells of the screen (128 px) each tree's image covers. **Only the trees whose image covers a cell the view touches get a canvas item**, taken from a shared pool and given back when they leave; just before each frame is drawn, only the cells that came or went are looked at. Before, every tree of every chunk within 1,024 px of the view went through the standing layer's depth sort each frame. The depth order is kept exactly: the items hang from a y-sorted node of the chunk's holder, placed at the tree's foot and indexed in the server's order, so trees, tall plants and bodies sort as before, ties included. Measured (3 km valley, 11,806 trees held in the heart of the big forest; the machine shared with other work, two alternated runs): the trees in the sort **1,179 → 408** for 362 on screen at zoom 0.4, **578 → 91** for 77 at zoom 1, **341 → 39** for 25 at zoom 2.5 (at the edge 424 → 154, 195 → 39, 103 → 20); the client's memory **203.6 → 159.3 MB** (3.7 KB a tree held), its nodes 16,338 → 4,893; draw calls and canvas items drawn unchanged (Godot already left out an item off screen, what the unseen trees cost was their sort), the frame's times within the noise, video memory unchanged; loading headless 4.3–4.8 → 4.3–4.4 s (chunk messages 1.22–1.31 → 1.18–1.19 s).
  - **Batching weighed against the depth order** (same day): the trees stay one canvas item each, because bodies and tall plants stand between them. A multimesh a species and chunk (technique 2 above) or one canvas item a chunk would draw a chunk's trees together, wrong at every seam between chunks, where crowns overlap, and around every body; bands of screen rows would keep the order only by splitting around each body. Technique 2 is withdrawn for anything sorted with the bodies. Technique 1, an atlas, was measured by drawing every tree with one image: the draw calls fall (350 → 57, dense wood, zoom 0.4), the trees' share of the processor's frame does not (about 1 ms either way); an atlas would also move pixels at the images' edges (mipmaps and compression blocks laid differently). Not done. What the trees cost now is the graphics card's: about 6 ms of a 14 ms frame in a dense wood at zoom 0.4 (trees shown against hidden, F6), the crowns drawn over one another. A polygon hugging each image (with room for the sway and the far mipmaps) would skip 13 to 21 % of their pixels (measured on the twenty images); kept for a later pass.
  - **The view's bookkeeping made event-driven, 11 November 2026** (lightness audit, pass 5; nothing changes on screen: captures before and after identical to the pixel in the forest, at the fog's edge east and in a corner, and again after walking over a chunk border and back, the fog's drift frozen for the comparison). What the world view used to work out every frame it now keeps as chunks come and go (`ViewLedger`): the bounds of the chunks held (grown on arrival, found again over the keys only when a chunk at their edge goes), the chunks waiting to build (kept nearest the camera first as they join, ordered again only when the camera has moved by a chunk), the see-through flags (worked out again only when the mouse, the piloted body's place, what lies behind the mouse or the trees held changed, and a few times a second regardless; the items near the mouse looked through only when the mouse or the items changed), the fog's and the standing layer's uniforms (sent only when their value changes). A chunk's neighbours are redrawn at once only within the camera's reach (the screen and a chunk around it); past it they are marked stale and redrawn when the camera comes, the same build then as at once. A built chunk hidden is **parked**, not freed, while the body stays within ten chunks of it (at most 96 parked; a teleport frees them all): sent again the same, it comes back as it was, its ground, trees and plants kept; its tiles leave the terrain grid meanwhile, so its neighbours draw their edge as before. Measured (3 km valley, the machine shared with other work): the world view's script time standing still **0.60 → 0.24 ms a frame** (0.59 → 0.23 at zoom 0.4); over a teleport its handlers **311 → 83 ms** and the network messages' handling 2,055 → 1,802 ms; a border crossing on foot **57 → 19 ground builds** the first time (19 new chunks, the 38 neighbour rebuilds deferred under the fog), **57 → 0** walking back (19 chunks restored) and 58 → 1 walking forth again; 19 parked chunks cost 4 MB. Next, from the same ledger: the chunks built only where the camera can reach (C1), the rest kept as data.
  - **Only what the screen reaches is built, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, C1 and C5; D08 amended the same day: the rule, the grace and the measures). The 361 chunks held stay data; the camera's reach, the screen and a chunk around it, decides which are built and which are released after a grace. The chunk is the unit of building as it is of transfer: what the screen reaches is a few dozen chunks whatever the view's size, so this is what keeps the client's building, nodes and memory proportional to the screen rather than to the view, and the view may grow with the land (D31) without the client paying for it. Nothing changes on screen (captures before and after identical to the pixel, the camera dragged fast at every zoom).
  - **The grounds built on workers, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, C3; technique (3) above, now done; D08 amended the same day: the margin by zoom, the budgets, the safety net and the measures). The heavy part of a chunk's build is pure data worked out on Godot's worker threads (`client/world/ground_build.gd`, bit for bit the in-line result: tested); the main thread gathers its input and lays the result within a budget, about 2 ms a chunk in all (its nodes 1.8, its ground 0.3) where it was 45 to 185 ms. Its cost no longer depends on what the chunk holds (a river, a brook, flowers): only on how many chunks the screen crosses.
  - **The fog drawn as rings, 10 November 2026** (lightness plan, B2 and B3; the study `content/ai-reserve/review/allegement-mesures/brouillard-gpu.md`; nothing visible changed, the project lead's fog of 9 November kept to the pixel). The two veils (fog C on the ground, fog B over everything) were quads of 2,048 × 2,048 tiles, shaded and blended over the whole screen every frame, though their alpha is exactly 0 everywhere more than half a tile inside the ground held (following the body, the first non-zero pixel lies 238 tiles from the screen); the far view, a third full-screen pass, lay under the opaque ground. Each veil is now a **ring** (`FarView.ring`: 12 vertices and 12 triangles in one `canvas_item_add_triangle_array`, the inner edges shared by index so no pixel is drawn twice nor missed on a seam) whose hole is the octagon inscribed in the rounded rectangle 0.75 tiles inside the ground held, where the shader is exactly nothing; built again only when that rectangle moves. The **far view leaves the same hole under the view's limit** once every chunk under it is held and built, none waiting, from the frame after (`FarView.limit_built`, `hole_drawn`), else the full quad as before; it is still sent and received (whether to stop is the project lead's call). The veils no longer need the far view's texture to exist nor for their size (`VEIL_REACH`: 1,024 tiles past the ground held, what the far view spans). The haze shader lives in `fog.gdshader`, one `Shader` for both veils, its constants as uniform defaults. Development: F7 cycles rings / quads / off (`SOUL_INVASION_FOG_DRAWING` starts a client in one), `SOUL_INVASION_FOG_TIME` pins the fog's drift so two captures compare. Measured on the project lead's machine (Iris Xe, 3072 × 1728, the GPU time of the whole frame, median of 600 frames in alternated runs, quads → rings → off): following the body the fog cost 1.4–1.7 ms a frame at every zoom and now costs nothing (rings = off within 0.1 ms); dragged to an edge's middle, its 1.75 / 3.38 / 2.34 ms at zooms 0.4 / 1 / 2.5 become 0.56 / 1.31 / 2.41 (−68 %, −61 %, equal: at zoom 2.5 the band is wider than the screen); in a rounded corner 1.94 / 2.81 / 2.26 become 1.21 / 2.05 / 1.72 (−38 %, −27 %, −24 %). Captures before and after (`content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/brouillard-anneaux/`): identical to the pixel at the body; at the edge and the corner 0.04–0.07 % of the pixels differ by one level of 255, scattered within the veil's band, because the tile coordinates the shader reads its noise at are interpolated from other triangles than before (the branch's own quads mode differs from `main` the same way); nothing visible, nothing avoidable short of the same triangles.
  - **The water made light, 11 November 2026** (plan d'allègement, B1; nothing changes on screen: captures at a river with banks and beaches, a ford, the 30-tile river and a brook, at zooms 0.4, 1 and 2.5, with the water's drift pinned (`SOUL_INVASION_WATER_TIME`, as `SOUL_INVASION_FOG_TIME`), identical to the pixel, zero differences). The water canvas's shader was the heaviest layer by far, about forty texture reads a pixel over every pixel of every chunk quad holding water, the land discarded only after the 17-sample beach search; measured at 6.3 / 8.4 / 8.9 ms of the frame at the river at zooms 0.4 / 1 / 2.5, the one layer whose cost grew with the zoom. It now does, per pixel, only what can show at that pixel's distance from the water's line, with bounds computed from its own uniforms: the land layers it never used are not read; land past the farthest edge the bank can have leaves after one read (two near a ford); deep water, where every term fed by the water's edge is saturated, skips the bank's gradient, wander, beaches, tufts and lap; the tufts are read only where the grass's outline can show; and the beach search follows the run of high noise outwards from the centre sample instead of reading the whole window (a run on one side of a point is shorter than the reach, so when a beach must be at least as long as the reach only the run through the point can be one): one read where the noise is low, most of the bank. Each shortcut replaces a computation only where its result is saturated or exactly zero, and the expressions that count are the same in the same order, which is why the picture is the same to the bit. Measured (the lead's machine, 3072 × 1728, two alternated runs, median of 320 frames): at the river with banks (tile 516, 1844), the water canvas's share of the GPU frame **34 / 52 / 56 % → 16 / 27 / 35 %** at zooms 0.4 / 1 / 2.5; in milliseconds at the same GPU clock (650–750 MHz), **5.2 / 6.7 / 9.3 → 1.9 / 3.4 / 4.0 ms** (−50 to −64 %); the one pass taken at a higher clock (1100–1300 MHz) read 2.8 / 6.0 / 6.0 ms before, so the shares are the figure to trust (`content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/eau-legere/README.md`). Tried and left: drawing the water canvas over its tiles' rows alone (parallelograms a band of rows) is not identical, texture coordinates being interpolated per triangle (±1/255 over 0.4 to 16 % of the water, and a few edge pixels flipping between earth and water); the idea stays for the three ground canvases together, judged with its pixels.
  - **The tree images follow the chunks held, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, E1; weight rule 4 of `09-visual-production.md`, now coded; nothing changes on screen once an image is in). A kind's images were read on first sight and kept for the session. Now each `Trees.Stand` holds its kinds from its making to its release (`Trees.hold` / `let_go`, a count a kind; a parked chunk keeps its stand, so it keeps its hold), the images of a kind are read on a worker thread (`ResourceLoader.load_threaded_request`) the first time a stand holds it, **one image at a time** whatever the kind (the main thread makes the video texture when it syncs the rendering, Godot's Compatibility renderer being single-threaded there: one a frame at most, instead of a kind's images piled into one frame), and freed thirty seconds (`Trees.UNLOAD_GRACE_S`: about 42 m of walking, where chunks come and go 288 m from the body) after the last stand let it go, so walking along a stand's edge does not read them again and again; the world going frees them all at once. A stand made while its images are read is built (its rectangles and cells) by the frame's `Trees.poll` once they are in, and shows nothing before: its trees appear together with their images, never a wrong one. Each image keeps the index it was given the first time, so a stand's indices stay good across a kind leaving and coming back. The tall plants and the small flora are one atlas (`ChunkView`, 6.8 MB) for every kind and stay as they were: splitting it would redo the atlas and its packing for a tenth of the trees' weight. F3 shows the kinds in memory, what they take (BC7 with mipmaps, from their size) against the textures' total, how many came and went, and the kinds being read. Measured (3 km valley, offscreen, the audit's capture harness; `content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/especes-par-region/`): captures before and after **identical to the pixel** (the forest at zooms 0.4 / 1 / 2.5, the camp at zoom 1); a walk of 210 tiles east from the river's bank (tile 1780, 850 → 1990, 850) and back, F3 on: **8 kinds / 127 MB of textures** at the start, **6 kinds / 114 MB** thirty seconds after the last chunk with an alder or a willow was let go (their 13 MB and nothing else), 8 kinds / 127 MB again on the way back; **no frame over 16 ms while a worker thread reads**, and the four images of the two kinds coming back land in four frames of 64 / 41 / 28 / 27 ms (the base frame 25 ms at the offscreen 40 Hz; asked for together, they landed in one frame of 142 ms: the texture upload, not the script, which takes 1.6 ms to build the eight stands); at login the eight kinds come in over the loading screen's frames, the loading screen waiting for the last read (7.2–7.8 s to load, as before). **The gain on today's valley is about nothing, as the plan said**: 8 of the 10 kinds have trees on it and 6 of them stand within 300 m wherever the body is, so the same 51 MB are in memory at login before and after, and only the alder and the willow (13 MB) leave when the body is more than 300 m from water; the gain is at the continent's scale (regions of different kinds) and before the growth stages, which would multiply what is kept for nothing.
  - **The water as meshes, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan; D36 amended the same day; the project lead's verdict on the look: "c'est vraiment super, c'est vraiment trop joli"). The water canvas's shader, even lightened (above), searched the bank at every pixel; the river is now two meshes a wet chunk (`WaterMeshes`, built on a worker thread, two to five texture reads a pixel, nothing searched), the brooks a mesh of ribbons, and the distance-field texture, its uploads and the ground shader's water branch are gone. Measured (the lead's machine, 3072 × 1728, F8: medians of 300 frames with and without the water, twice, alternated; today's water measured on the prototype branch where both still drew): the water's share of the GPU frame at a river with banks **29–33 % → 14–19 %**, at a ford **45–48 % → 17–23 %**, at the great river at zoom 0.4 **43–46 % → 25–28 %** (the prototype's one-layer water without streaks had read 3 to 8 times less in milliseconds; the project lead chose the two layers and the streaks, 2 to 3 times less); in milliseconds at the GPU clock the old water ran at (600–700 MHz), 3.2–5.9 → 1.1–1.7, 6.1–6.7 → 1.8–2.2 and 3.6–4.0 → about 1.5 ms; the measures at a higher clock (1100–1350 MHz) read 0.6–0.65, 1.2–1.5 and 0.8–1.0 ms. A wet chunk's meshes build in 35–100 ms on a worker (the machine's load decides) and take 2–4 ms of the main thread. The meshes' video memory: **16.7 → 6.2 MB** held around the river with banks (÷2.7), **14.5 → 4.5 MB** at the ford (÷3.2), **25.3 → 6.7 MB** at the great river (÷3.8), the "before" being what the same cells weighed as the prototype drew them (quarter-tile quads, float colours and texture coordinates, 32-bit indices); the ÷4 asked for comes only where the deep water dominates, since the bank's band keeps its quarter-tile lattice for the ink, by packing the colours as bytes (the distance and the width stay floats: the ink lines come from them), dropping the water mesh's texture coordinates (its shader takes the point of the map from the vertex), 16-bit indices, the quarter-tile quads as strips, and the deep water merged into half-tile quads where its vertices' values are, to a byte, what the four quarter quads would interpolate (a merged quad beside a finer one keeps the shared vertex: no T-junction, no crack). Captures before and after at the three places, the water and fog pinned, in `content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/eau-finale/`.
  - **The trees drawn as their tight outlines, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, B6; the plan allowed "quasi identique (interpolation)"). A tree was drawn as its image's whole rectangle, widened by the shader for the wind's bend, and 43 to 66 % of that rectangle is clear: every one of those pixels still ran the sway shader, read the texture and discarded, the largest remaining cost of the graphics card in the forest heart. Each image now has a **tight outline** (`TreeOutline`, `client/art/trees/outlines.json`, written offline by `client/tools/tree_outlines.gd` and to be run again whenever a tree image changes; the test `tree outlines hold every texel` fails until it is): a y-monotone polygon of at most 16 vertices around every texel the shader can draw, worked out from the texture **as the card holds it** (the compressed texture with its mipmaps, levels 0 to 4: the farthest zoom in a 1280 x 720 window reads level 4; its compression leaks a little alpha beside the drawn texels at each level, so each level is read on its own), each texel grown by the texel the linear filter reaches, the rows widened by the wind's largest bend there (`(SWAY + 1.5 GUST) * k^2`, `k` from `STILL`, now `Trees` constants the world view sets and the shader's defaults match) and a pixel of safety. `Trees.Stand` draws the outline scaled to the tree's rectangle as a triangle array (triangulated once per image; its UVs are the image's own coordinates, past 0..1 in the room left for the bend), and the shader no longer widens anything. The outlines cover **52 to 74 %** of the widened rectangle (a tree's clear pixels drawn: −26 to −48 %). The shadows are two rects of a shade texture, untouched (polygons since, B5, below). Captures at the forest heart, zooms 0.4, 1 and 2.5: with the wind off, 6 to 7 % of the pixels differ by 1/255 and under 0.06 % by 2 to 4/255, none more (the UVs interpolated over other triangles); with the wind pinned at one moment, the pictures differ over the crowns because the gust is read at the vertices and interpolated (the vertices moved); the branch drawn with the whole rectangles again under the same pinned wind differs from the outlines as much, the gust interpolated between 4 corners or 16 vertices; no leaf is clipped: the test holds every texel at the largest bend, and the bend is bounded at every vertex, so everywhere between. GPU at the forest heart (five alternated pairs of 300 frames, vsync off, the machine shared; the two quiet pairs agree): **13.4 → 11.1 ms at zoom 0.4, 13.0 → 10.9 at 1, 11.4 → 9.5 at 2.5** (medians of the three sampled pairs; −1 to −2 ms in each quiet pair), draw calls 350 → 418 at 0.4 (polygons do not batch as rects do), render CPU unchanged.
  - **The ground drawn by its rows, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, B9). Each terrain's canvas drew one quad over its whole chunk and its shader discarded the tiles of the other terrains, after reading the index: a chunk with a glade ran the ground shader two or three times on every pixel. Now a chunk's build (`GroundBuild.rows_of`, on the worker) gives each canvas the tiles it draws as rectangles of whole rows (a row's runs, a run going on from the row above extending its rectangle; a rectangle of tiles is a parallelogram on screen, the union of its diamonds), drawn as one triangle array: the same draw calls, no pixel of another terrain's tile shaded; the index test stays as a safety net and for the forest's spill. The ground shader also reads less where nothing of it can show, an exact bound as before: the forest floor's sister is read only where it shows or a bare pixel takes it (the floor's `bare_keep`), and on the forest's spill, where its own floor does not reach, only the shade is drawn and no layer is read. Captures at the forest heart against `main`, zooms 0.4, 1 and 2.5: the shader's part changes nothing beyond the run-to-run noise (the rows alone give the same differences); the triangles' part moves the textures' interpolation by a rounding, as for the trees' outlines (0.04 / 0.31 / 1.85 % of pixels, nearly all 1/255, 78 pixels above 4/255 at 2.5, at most 11/255, invisible). GPU at the forest heart, one pass each on a shared machine: 14.4 → 12.9 ms at zoom 0.4, 12.6 → 12.2 ms at zoom 1.
  - **The finest level of the tree images only up close, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, E2; the plan asked for "identical to the pixel under the threshold, proven"). The tree images were held with all their mip levels, and below about zoom 0.7 on the lead's screen the card never reads the finest one, three quarters of their weight. Each image is now read **without its finest level** (`Trees.COARSE`, level 1: half its width and height) by `Trees.read_image`, straight from the imported `.ctex` (its header, then the levels finest first, each padded to the 4 × 4 blocks): the coarse image is the whole texture's own chain of BC7 blocks from its second level, so wherever the card would not read the finest level it reads the very same texels; it is made an `ImageTexture` given the imported texture's size (`set_size_override`), so a tree's rectangle, its outline and the shader's `TEXTURE_PIXEL_SIZE` are as before. A kind gets its finest level back only while a tree of it has a canvas item (`Stand._show` / `_hide` count them a kind) and `Trees.fine_read` says the card may read that level: the level of detail it picks for the kind's largest tree (its image's padded height in texels over the tree's height on screen, `SIZE_JITTER` over, × the zoom × the screen's stretch, plus the shader's `mip_bias`) is under 1 + `FINE_MARGIN` (0.15). On the lead's 3072 × 1728 screen a kind is whole from zoom 0.61–0.66 (the card's own threshold, without the margin, about 0.68–0.70), so coarse at the wheel's steps up to 0.6 and whole from 0.8; on a 1280 × 720 window, from zoom 0.94–0.99. `FINE_GRACE_S` (10 s) after a kind stops needing it (zoomed out, or out of view), it is read coarse again. The level changes by `ImageTexture.set_image`, which keeps the texture's RID: no tree is drawn again, nothing on screen changes when a kind softens, and when one sharpens only the few frames before its images are in show the coarse level (one image a frame, after the kinds arriving). The reads go through the WorkerThreadPool, one image at a time as before (`ResourceLoader.load_threaded_request` is gone; a file `read_image` cannot cut is loaded whole, as before); the loading screen also waits for the kinds being sharpened, so the first view is as sharp as the next. F3 shows the levels held (the kinds whole marked `*`, the kinds `sharpening`). Measured at the forest heart (tile 2268, 1156; offscreen, 3072 × 1728, the audit's harness; `content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/mip-fin/`): the six kinds held there **38.2 → 9.6 MB** at zoom 0.4 (Godot's textures total 109.0 → 80.3 MB), **25.9 MB** at 0.8 and at 2.5 (the three kinds on screen whole: oak, linden, hazel); the plan's 68 → 17 MB for the ten kinds follows (a quarter). Captures against main at zooms 0.4, 0.8 and 1.0: **the trees identical to the pixel** at all three (at 0.8 and 1.0 once the kinds on screen are whole); 210 and 217 pixels of the ground between the crowns differ by 1/255 at 0.4 and 0.8, none at 1.0, none on a tree. The arrival of a kind (two kinds, four images, one a frame) cost frames of 27–28 ms against main's 31–32 ms around a 26 ms median offscreen, both close to the noise here (E1's 64 ms was not reproduced in this run); what is sent to the card on arrival is a quarter (0.9 instead of 3.6 MB an oak image), by construction. Sharpening the three kinds on screen when zooming in (six whole images) showed no frame over 31 ms.
  - **A tree without images of its own is drawn with a stand-in's, 16 November 2026** (the six fruit and nut trees, planted for the vivarium's food before their art: wild cherry, mulberry and wild pear drawn as the wild apple, walnut as the oak, sweet chestnut as the linden, wild fig as the hazel; `Trees.STAND_INS`). A tree takes its tile, so a kind planted with no image would stand in the way unseen; drawn with a stand-in's images at its own height, it is seen. The table is the client's, for display only, and a kind leaves it by having images of its own.
  - **The tall plants as stands, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, B10; nothing changes on screen). A plant from 0.9 m (brambles, elders...) was a node of its own in its chunk's holder, every one of a chunk near the screen in the standing layer's depth sort each frame. A chunk's tall plants are now a **`PlantStand`**, the trees' pattern: packed arrays (each one's foot, rectangle, atlas region) and the screen's cells their images cover; only those whose image covers a cell the view touches get a canvas item, from the trees' pool. Their items hang from a y-sorted node after the trees', placed at the foot and indexed in the server's order, so trees, tall plants and bodies sort as before, ties included.
  - **The trees' shadows as polygons, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, B5; the same look). A tree's two shadows were two rects of a radial shade texture, their clear corners blended for nothing. Each is now a 16-sided polygon of the ellipse's area (`Trees.shadow_polygon`), its opacity in its vertices' colours (full in the middle, the gradient's knee at 0.55 of the radius, nothing at the rim), and a chunk's shadows are one triangle array, drawn in the same order: no texture read, a command a chunk instead of two a tree.
  - **The art kept in git as lossless WebP, 12 November 2026** (lightness plan, E3; weight rule 5 of `09-visual-production.md`). The shipped images under `content/art/` (trees, the flora's atlas, decorations, ground and water textures) were PNG; they are now lossless WebP, written with the colour under transparent pixels kept: every image decodes to the same RGBA bytes (`client/tools/webp_same_pixels.py`: 71 images, 0 mismatches) and Godot's imported textures are identical byte for byte, with the same import settings and identifiers. Nothing changes in the game, its memory or its download (it ships the imported textures); the repository's images went from 75.1 MB to 52.2 MB (−30.5 %), and every future version of the art is as much lighter in the history. The one greyscale image stays PNG (WebP would make it RGB, three times its texture).
  - **A video-memory budget per category, 12 November 2026** (lightness plan, E4; weight rule 6 of `09-visual-production.md`, with the table). About **184 MB a season** on the graphics card, the current season only, at the worst place and any zoom, measured by F3: trees 64, plants and decorations 16, ground 24, water 16, bodies and animals 24, buildings and items 24, UI, fields and effects 16 (MB). Today about 55 MB at the forest heart (trees 25.9 from zoom 0.8). Every new image states its category and stays within it, or the lead moves the line.
  - **The trees' budget per screen, a test, 19 November 2026** (step 4, lot 14; the biomes study of the same day, approach A, approved by the lead). `server/sim/tests/tree_budget.rs` places the trees of sample regions as the server does (the game's 3 km valley and two windows of the atlas; a biome with kinds of its own adds its regions), lays the client's screens over them (1280 × 720 canvas, a camera every 64 tiles, zooms 0.4, 1.5 and 2.5), counts the kinds each screen shows and each view holds (19 × 19 chunks) and multiplies them by each kind's bytes as the card holds them, from the image sizes the art tool writes (`client/art/trees/outlines.json`); a kind with no image yet is weighed as one of its shown height at the shipped sharpness, so a species or a biome is judged before its art is made. The client's rules of today are mirrored (E1, E2: the view's kinds coarse, a kind shown whole where the card may read its finest level on the lead's 3072 × 1728 screen). It fails over three lines: **the trees' video memory 64 MB at any place and zoom** (E4); **an upload of 1.4 MB a frame** (about 2 ms of the main thread at the measured 1.5–1.9 ms a MB), checked today on a kind's arrival (the largest coarse image, 1.33 MB) and printed for a kind sharpening (5.31 MB, wild cherry; the ash's 5.23 MB costs 9.9 ms), over the line until the uploads are paced by bytes; **7 screens of tree outlines on 99 screens in 100** (the study's 7 came from the audit's 6.1–6.4 screens of the images' rectangles at the forest heart; the card shades the outlines, about 0.72 of the rectangles since B6, 4.6 screens there; in rectangles a twentieth of the wooded screens are over 7 today, in outlines one screen of the valley at zoom 2.5, 7.6). The table it prints, today: kinds on screen at zoom 0.4 / 1.5 / 2.5 at most 13 / 10 / 9 (the valley), the view holding 10 to 14 kinds; the trees' video memory at most 15.7 / 51.7 / 48.8 MB; outlines at most 5.6 / 6.8 / 7.6 screens, 5.9 on 99 in 100. The model checks against the audit's forest heart: 6.3–6.5 screens of rectangles (6.1–6.4 measured), 26.4 MB (25.9 measured).
  - **A crowd costs what the screen shows, 19 November 2026** (step 4, A6; nothing changes on screen). The client was told every body within about 150 m (D32) and walked each of them every frame, gave each a node redrawn every frame, and looked through all of them under the mouse: its cost followed the bodies in range, about 49 chunks, where the screen shows 8 to 15. Now only the bodies the screen may show are walked and drawn (`BodyCrowd`): the area watched is the screen grown by 512 px, looked at again when the screen leaves its middle and when a body is told a walk. A walk is looked at leg by leg, each leg's screen box grown by the body's figure and by what is left of its gap to its walk: a body whose current leg meets the area is walked on every frame; one whose later leg meets it waits, untouched, for the tick it sets off on that leg (still well off the screen) and is walked on from that frame; one whose walk never meets it is left as it is until told again. Walks go by the clock, so a body left aside is brought exactly to where the last frame would have had it the moment something reads it (a new walk told, its chunk leaving). A body's node exists only while it is shown; it is moved without a redraw and redrawn only when what it shows changes (piloted, asleep, what it holds). The piloted body is always walked on. The range told (150 m) is unchanged: a body off the screen is still known and appears where it should be when the camera pans. Measured headless (1,000 bodies walking in a 150 m disc, zoom 0.4, 4 alternated runs): the world view's script time **9.3–12.2 → 2.3–2.9 ms a frame** (185 bodies watched); captures before and after (400 walking bodies, panned and jumped onto bodies off the screen, zooms 1 and 0.4, the thicket at fruit 0, 4 and 8) identical to the pixel, 13 of 13.
  - **A chunk's low plants and berries as one triangle array, 19 November 2026** (step 4, A6; audit F7). The plant layer of a chunk drew each low plant and each berry as a rect command: at peak fruit the renderer re-sent every one of them each frame (about 450,000 in the thicket at zoom 0.4) and kept the buffers grown for them after the fruit was gone. The layer is now one `canvas_item_add_triangle_array` (`PlantLayer.arrays_of`): a quad a draw, in the same order, so each draws over those before it as before, its region of the atlas worked out with the renderer's own 32-bit rounding; built on the worker with the ground, or on the main thread when only the fruit changed, and uploaded once. Plants and their berries stay one element: a berry lies in depth between plants, so drawing them apart would change which shows over which; a fruit change already touched only that element and the trees' fruit. Measured (the thicket, zoom 0.4, 3072 × 1728, fruit forced on every tree and plant, 2 alternated runs each): at fruit 8 the render CPU **7.95–8.09 → 3.40–3.63 ms**, the GPU 11.7–13.6 → 8.4–9.2 ms, the renderer's buffers **166 → 107–111 MB**, video memory 273 → 214–218 MB, the client's memory 212 → 154 MB; back at fruit 0 the buffers fall to 29–34 MB where they stayed at 152. The picture is the same to the eye but not to the pixel: 0.01 % of the pixels differ at fruit 0 (up to 11 levels of 255), 0.15 % at fruit 8 (up to 49), single pixels at the edges of the small sprites; most likely because the renderer clamps a rect's sampling to its region, which changes the mipmap level chosen at a sprite's edge pixels, and a triangle array has no such clamp.
  - **The land baked into screen pages, 20 November 2026** (the rendering study of 19 November, I2, `content/ai-reserve/review/reflexion-2026-11-19/3-rendu.md`; the project lead's choice: "garder ce qu'on a et le réduire", the same picture for less of the card's time, to free it for the bodies and animals to come, for richer grounds and for ordinary computers). The camera lies on whole screen pixels (`WorldView._snap_camera`), the zoom moves by nine steps with no glide (`Zoom.STEPS`) and the land's shader has no clock (`TIME` is read for the water only), so at a given zoom the land is one fixed picture that only slides by whole pixels. The grass's and the forest's canvases (`ChunkView.GroundCanvas`) and the far view under them (`FarView`, z −20: nothing lies between them on the screen) now draw on a canvas of their own (`GroundPages`), which a grid of **512-pixel pages** sees, each a viewport shifted by a whole page (`PageLedger`: the screen and a ring of 256 pixels around it, 48 pages on a 3072 × 1728 screen). Each page is rendered once and the world view shows the pages' pictures where the canvases were (z −7), copied as they are; the water, the banks, the brooks, the fog's veils, the plants, the shadows, the trees and the bodies stay live, in the same order. A page is rendered again only when the camera brings it into the ring (a few a frame, nearest first, ahead of the motion; one the screen already shows at once), when the ground under it is laid again (a chunk's ground, its terrain index, its slopes: D44), and every page on a zoom step, a teleport (the grid lies elsewhere) or a change of the land's look (a material's dial, the turf set, the night's modulate, the far view sent again). The far view's hole changing renders nothing: it is cut only where the near ground is opaque. **A page is opaque at 10 bits a channel**, the screen's own format (Godot's opaque render targets are RGB10A2): a transparent page is 8 bits, and rounding the land to 8 bits before the screen rounds it to 10 moved one channel by one level on 15 % of the pixels; opaque, with the far view inside, nothing is rounded twice. Same picture (captures live and paged in the same run, `SOUL_INVASION_GROUND_PAGES=0` drawing the land live as before, the lead's vivarium world, 3072 × 1728): a meadow at zooms 2.5 and 0.4, **0 pixels differ**; a slope at a forest edge, 1.0 % of the pixels at 2.5 and 0.8 % at 0.4 move by one level (at most 2, and 10 on 12 pixels of the slope turf at 2.5), and the valley's meadow at 0.4, 2.1 % by one level: floating-point rounding in the GPU (derivatives taken over 2 × 2 pixel blocks, which a page aligns differently from the screen, and vertex positions computed from another origin), the same rounding that changes the live picture on many more pixels each time the camera moves one pixel. The gain (the card's frame time, `viewport_get_measured_render_time_gpu`, four alternations of 120 frames, live → pages, medians): meadow **6.4 → 1.4 ms** at 2.5 and **7.9 → 1.2 ms** at 0.4; slope and forest edge **10.3 → 2.5 ms** at 2.5 and **11.6 → 3.9 ms** at 0.4. A frame where nothing is rendered again reads the pages and shades no ground; a zoom step renders every page once, about a live frame's ground. **The cost: 96 MB of video memory** (48 pages, each a 1 MB colour target and the 1 MB depth-stencil buffer Godot's GL renderer gives every render target), a budget line of its own beside the ground's 24 MB (E4 above; `09-visual-production.md` rule 6). **Settled with the lead on 20 November 2026: accepted.** The cost follows the screen alone, never the map, the kinds or the content: 2 MB a page, (⌈(width + 512) / 512⌉ + 1) × (⌈(height + 512) / 512⌉ + 1) pages, so 20 pages and 40 MB at 1366 × 768, 30 and 60 MB at 1920 × 1080, 48 and 96 MB at 3072 × 1728, 70 and 140 MB at 3840 × 2160. On an integrated GPU it is taken from the main memory (96 MB of 8 GB, about 1 %), and what limits such a card is the pixels it shades each frame, which the pages cut. **The rule that comes with it: nothing that changes often goes into the pages.** What moves or changes by the second (the water, and later footprints, a fire, the daylight, snow melting tile by tile) is a layer drawn over them; a slow change (a season's colour) renders the pages again once. A page rendered every frame loses the gain. **Kept in reserve**, if video memory ever becomes the limit: no ring (35 pages, 70 MB at 3072 × 1728); 8-bit pages (half the colour, one level on some pixels); no depth-stencil buffer, half the cost, but the Compatibility renderer gives one to every render target unless overridden, and the override is not offered to scripts: an engine change, not ours to make.
  - **The server stays proportional to what is used, not to the map**: the world is regenerated from its seed and only what changed is kept (a plant picked, a tree felled), placement is computed chunk by chunk when a chunk is sent, and path searches become hierarchical (D10, D11) as the map grows.

  - **20 November 2026 (audit F8)**: `ShoreField` and `ResourceLoader.load_threaded_request`, named above, are gone from the client (the bank meshes of 11 November, the worker reads of E2); the paragraphs stay as the record of what was tried and why.

### D40 — One scale for the world: true under 3 m, eased above
- **Reason**: the project lead's call (9 November 2026): the world keeps true proportions, as far as the screen allows. Each thing had had its own scale (the body a third of a person, trees a fifth of a real tree, plants at their true height), and reeds stood twice the body's height.
- **Consequences**: everything under 3 m is drawn at its true height (a body 1.7 m, a reed 2 m, a dandelion 20 cm, a mushroom 8 cm); above, heights ease towards reality without reaching it, 3 m × (h / 3 m)^0.5 (a 22 m oak shows about 8 m, nearly five times a body). ^0.7 (about 12 m) then ^0.6 (about 10 m) were tried on 9 November 2026: the trees made the land look cramped at the farthest zoom; smaller trees give the sense of space the project lead asked for, so tall things stay tall without filling the screen. One function on the client (`Scale.shown`); content gives true heights. Small plants are drawn a little brighter so the grass does not swallow them. The trees move to their true heights with this rule in a following step, with their spacing, since bigger crowns mean fewer trees and cost more to draw (D39). (10 November 2026) Their spacing is now the grid's (D37): one tree a 4 m cell, the same for every kind; the room each kind kept from its drawn height is gone, with its floats. A wider spacing for the big kinds (an oak taking a nested 8 m cell) is a question for the project lead.
- **Amended on 10 November 2026: big trees and small ones, a closed canopy.** Shown at the eased height, a big tree (oak, pine, ash, linden: `big` in `species.ron`) has a wide crown. The big kinds stand on a grid of 6.25 m cells (trunks nudged up to 1.8 m: in the heart their crowns nearly touch, the ground glimpsed between them), the small ones on a 3 m grid between them, never within 2.4 m of a big trunk; a narrow cell that would hold a big kind holds an undergrowth tree (hazel, now and then yew) 42 times in a hundred. The chance of a cell follows the woods' density (D37: thin at the edge, thickening to the heart): 6 thousandths a point of density on the narrow grid, 8 on the wide one (its cells are larger, so the edge keeps as many big trees a square metre), and 12 more a point past the forest's density, at most 92 %. The client still varies each tree's size by up to 12 % either way from its place.

- **Amended on 18 November 2026: the small things drawn twice their height** (the project lead's call, after a test sheet on the game's grass). At their true height, a chickweed or a lamb's lettuce of 10 cm was 15 pixels at the usual zoom, smaller than a tuft of the grass, and an ink line or livelier colours alone did not make it seen. Everything up to 30 cm is now drawn at twice its height (a mushroom of 8 cm shows 16 cm, a dandelion 40); from 30 cm to 1 m the shown height grows more slowly, from 60 cm to 1 m, so a taller thing is never drawn shorter than a smaller one; from 1 m to 3 m nothing changes (a body, a reed). The thresholds that read a plant's height (brighter below 70 cm, standing among the bodies from 90 cm) read its true height, so the same plants keep them. The small plants (30 cm and under, mushrooms aside) are also made with livelier colours (saturation × 1.3, brightness × 1.08) and a 2 px ink line (`flora_art.py`). A later idea of the project lead, not built: a style where each leaf and fruit of a small plant has its own line, so it reads as leaves rather than a green mass.

### D41 — The vivarium: a world without players, under glass, before the game
- **Reason**: the project lead's decisions of 12 and 13 November 2026. Before players, robots or the project lead himself, we must see whether NPCs alone, starting from the Stone Age, live, discover, quarrel, organise and move forward, and for good reasons. The game has no food, no animals and no water to drink yet; a band living decades in minutes tests the world's rules faster and more honestly than any play session. This replaces "the vivarium will come long after", said on 12 November: it is now the road through milestone 2 (`05`). The plan, decided, is in `content/ai-reserve/review/vivarium/PLAN.md`; its two later checks override its first order of work.
- **Consequences**:
  - **A program apart, nothing thrown away**: `vivarium`, in this repository, with no server and no client, depending only on `sim`. Whatever it needs (the brain, knowledge, births, seasons, content) is written in the simulation and serves the game; the body rules changed for it hold for the game too and are shown in the client (D34, D35). It reads the same content (D27), separates a map seed from a run seed (D24) and shows its own speed in years per minute.
  - **Small to test, never false**: every compression (nature's yield, map size, pace) is a **factor named in the content**, with its reason, shown in every report, and meant to come back towards reality as the world grows. No rule depends on the size of this valley. The map is somewhat larger than the current valley, 6 to 12 km a side, chosen after measuring what a larger map costs the generator. **Amended on 14 November 2026 — the map is a zone of the atlas**: the project lead chose zone 2 of seed 14's continent, 2,176 m a side, cut from the atlas at the tile (`sim::plan::vivarium`; `05-first-version.md`, lot 0).
  - **People**: one band of about forty, families aged 0 to 60, then two tribes of 100 to 150 on different lands (forest and river; hills and meadows). Willpower is drawn for each, mostly modest, with a thin share of strong personalities. While there is a single band, **strangers arrive now and then as spouses**, or the band marries cousins within three generations.
  - **Point A**, day one: they know fire, knapping, hafting an axe and a spear with rawhide thongs and sinew, butchering, gathering, hunting with the spear, fishing by hand or spear, sleeping in a rock shelter; they wear clothes; a hide tied as a bundle is their only container (D35). They do not know cord, containers, drying and smoking, tanning, tents or huts, traps, or the winter store.
  - **Point B, written in advance**: at least six of nine criteria, at least three of them technical (cord then a container; drying or smoking; a winter store made in autumn and eaten in winter; a built shelter in a seasonal camp; a famine then a dated answer; a technique passed over two generations and another lost; a chief recognized after a store exists; two lands, two peoples discovering different things first; the meeting of the two tribes and what it changed), each with its reason in the chronicle, on at least twelve of twenty judging seeds. Guard rails: no band dies out in more than a quarter of the seeds, none discovers everything in under ten years, three survivable shocks (a hard winter, an epidemic, a fire).
  - **Not fooling ourselves**: tune on seeds 1 to 20, judge on 21 to 40 and on a second map; anchor births, deaths and yields on real data, compressed by the named factors; an **audit of reasons**: remove the cause (no winter, no linden), the effect must vanish.
  - **The world it needs**: the flora moves from the server into the simulation as a **dated state of the world that regrows**, with seasons and fruit years; dead wood returns over the years; **actions that last** (today everything but walking takes one tick); gathering limited by the time of work, a yield per hour, not by the count of plants; **a small herd of deer from the first run** (meat, fat, hides to remake clothes); the **simple fire first** (lit, kept by adding wood, warming, cooking), the full model of processes before discovery; **rock shelters before true caves**, generated near water (the map has none today), their images made only once their shape is validated with placeholders.
  - **The report**: a web page (map, curves, a sorted chronicle, a page per person, three lives followed), built from events that say what and how much (eaten, rotted). Every rule has its test in the CI; invariants each simulated year (food and population conserved, every death has a cause, every piece of knowledge an origin); the same seeds give the same report; the twenty-seed batches run outside the CI.
  - **Later**: the client as a spectator, full beliefs and rumours, speech, souls and players; then robots that play like players (lie a lot, hunt everything, go fast), then the project lead, then others.

  - **Judged small, measured whole, 15 November 2026** (the project lead's question after the vivarium's canal-like river and its empty plains, two defects found only by walking in one cell of a 2,097 km continent all drawn by the same generator, atlas → region → window: "how do we make sure the whole continent will not have the same problems?"; the answer agreed: measure, don't look). **Every generator rule is judged on a tiny zone by the lead, then measured on sampled windows of the whole continent before merging**: the invariants of `plan::net` (about a hundred windows of one atlas cell, 2 km, stratified by biome, river size, coast and relief, the same cells every run: tree densities by biome, no straight reach, breathing banks, no sharp bend, a ford and a path at every atlas crossing, a fringe of trees at the forest's edge, no tree in or by the water, no pit and no step over 1.7 m off a declared cliff, every brook reaching water) and the sheets of `windows_sheet` (24 windows a biome, for the lead's eye). A window that fails is reported with its cell as a finding, never fixed in the measuring change; what the net does not see yet is listed in `06-open-questions.md` (generator blind spots).

  - **One page per family; strangers only for a lone band, 22 November 2026** (lot 3 C, PR 2; the proposal `content/ai-reserve/review/vivarium/lot-3/proposition-3C.md` validated by the project lead on 21 November 2026; the page per family asked on 14 November, PLAN-v3 §4.2). The report's **page per person becomes a page per family**: a couple and its children under sixteen, named by the mother ("the family of person 7"); a single adult is a family of one; a child leaves at sixteen for a family of its own and a man for his wife's when he marries, each move a line in the family left and in the one joined. Each page gives its members with age, sex and state (pregnant, nursing, days thin and weak), its births, its deaths with cause and day, what its members gathered and the mean fat reserve of its members alive, then each member's own page under it. The family is **worked out by the report** from the kinship the bodies carry (D49, `vivarium::Report::families`): `sim` keeps no family id and the world keeps no dead; the registry of mother, father, spouse, children, day and cause of death lives in the report, built from the events (`BodyBorn`, `Coupled`, `PregnancyLost`, `BodyDied`). The departure of a run pairs the grown by the sim's own spouse rule (`World::couple_all`, day one) and gives every child and infant a mother and a father among the camp's couples, the infant's nurse being its mother (`World::set_parents`). The report also counts, **per tribe and per year**: the population and its growth, the births, the deaths by cause with the season of each death of hunger, the mean interval between a birth and its mother's birth before it, the children born in the run who reached fifteen, and **the fertile women without a possible spouse** (16 to 42, no living spouse, no unwed man of the tribe of 16 to 50 who is not their kin). **Strangers arriving as spouses hold for the lone band only**: with two tribes the elsewhere is the other tribe and the meeting comes from exploration (V5), not from a generator or a third tribe; in twenty years inbreeding does not bite (the first-born are sixteen in year 17) and D49's kinship bar is enough. Past 20 % of fertile women without a possible spouse on a seed, the question reopens with a number; a newcomer from outside the zone every N years stays the fallback, with no code in advance. Reason: a death has its place in a family, not on a page of its own, and what the lead reads of a tribe is who lives with whom; a stranger generator would hide the one measure that says whether one is needed.

  - **The report is data, JSON, plus a page, 22 November 2026** (the project lead's decision of the laboratory, D52, the same day: its reading screens must read data, never parse a page of 3 to 5 MB). Beside each HTML page `X.html` the vivarium writes `X.json`, the same report as data: `meta` (the format marker, the settings, the world, the camps, the timing), `years` (each tribe's year in numbers with its food), `found` (the trials by the kind of the step), `people` (a line per person with its lines of life only), `curves` (the samples the page already thins to 1,200 points), `presence` (where each tribe's living stood, counted once a day at noon by cells of 32 tiles, the one thing sampled for the data alone: the lead wants the lab to show the movements and where a new tribe lives) and `map` (the page's picture, so the lab draws on it); the homes moved are listed by year (`years[].home_moves`); nothing per tick, and the chronicle stays on the page. Every key, with its unit and since when, is in `docs/10-vivarium-data.md`, and a test walks them both ways. The page is for the lead's eyes; the lab (D52) reads the data and nothing else. Deterministic but for the timing and the run's date, as the page.
  - **Amended on 23 November 2026 — the report tells the fire and the common memory (D55; lot 4, PR 2).** The data file gains `records[]` (the common memory at the end: number, kind, day, witness, place or way, kept), `hearth[]` (the nights each hearth still holds: day, present, what was told by whom), `people[].believes[]` (a body's places at the end: the record, seen or heard from whom, which day), `people[].will_start`, `found[].heard.*` (trials from hearsay, copies, rumours dispelled), `found[].by_will[]` (trials and kept by band of the will), `years[].knowledge` (places known by the tribe's living, by hearsay only, accesses heard), `years[].fire` (nights, told, heard, dispelled), `years[].food.stored_kj` (the food the tribe carries at noon: mean, minimum, end; the lead's metric) and `years[].ways.stock_kj` (quartiles); `meta.settings.fire` and `meta.memory`. The chronicle gets one sentence per trial told at the fire ("at the fire, person 14 says its home moved did it good; person 7 and person 9 believed it") and one per rumour dispelled (the first 1,000 of a run: a camp of 25 dispels about 27 a day, mostly at a look in passing, and written one by one they made a twenty-year file 17 MB); places and waters told are counted, not written as lines. The lab's run page gains **Ce que la tribu sait** (places known and food carried, year by year), **Qui a osé quoi** (each body's will at the start and the end, its trials, kept, heard; then by band), **Ce que X croit** (in each life), **Qui a dit quoi à qui** (the nights kept) and **Rumeurs**; its plans gain `with_and_without_fire` (`-feu` / `-sansfeu`, the latter with `--no-fire`) beside `with_and_without_trials`. Reason: the lead's metric of the fire is the food kept and who dared what; the page must show them, and a control must isolate the fire.
  - **Amended on 23 November 2026 — the tribe's places year by year, the rumours counted whole (lab 5, the run page laid out).** Each `years[].knowledge` gains `where`: the places the tribe's living know at the end of that year, as `[x, y, seen]` (1 seen with their own eyes, 0 only heard at the fire), at most `KNOWN_PLACES_KEPT` = 1,000 a tribe a year (a 20-year run of two tribes writes about 12,000 places, about 0.2 MB of its 5.5 MB). The lab's map draws them year by year, so "why they are there" is read for the year under the cursor, not only for the end. `found[].heard.dispelled` now counts every rumour dispelled (the sum of `years[].fire.dispelled`); until this day it counted only the rumours kept in `rumours[]`, at most 1,000, and a 20-year run read 1,000 where 59,340 had been dispelled. Reason: the project lead's reading of the run page, 23 November 2026 (lab 5): "pourquoi ils sont là" year by year, and the defect of the capped count found by the first try. One defect is left for a later pull request: when two bodies of a tribe know the same place, the last one read decides whether it was seen or heard, so `heard_only` can depend on the order of the bodies.
  - **Amended on 23 November 2026 — the page is minimal, the lab reads the rest (lab 6; the brief `content/ai-reserve/review/vivarium/lab/BRIEF-lab-6-one-page.md`).** The project lead reads the lab's run page, which now holds everything the vivarium's page held; the vivarium's page becomes a **minimal technical page** (the settings, the counts at the end, the map, the timing, a pointer to the lab and the data), about 0.8 MB where a twenty-year page weighed 8 MB, and `--page full` writes the long page of before (the chronicle is on it alone). The data file gains, with no change of `format` (new keys only): `meta.factors[]` (every value of `content/needs.ron` by its path, `key` and `value`, so the lab's factor cards follow the content and never a list in the lab), `bearers` (the food map: per chunk, the bearers in season in each of the four seasons and the three most numerous kinds, placed as the world places them for the bodies, **only around the tribes' land**: the chunks a tribe's living stood in at some noon and `BEARERS_MARGIN_CHUNKS` = 2 around, some 4 s after a run where the whole world cost 45 s) and `relief` (the ground's height in decimetres every twice the picture's scale). Reason: one page to read, in the lab, where it can load its heavy pieces on demand; the data must carry what that page draws (D52).
  - **Amended on 25 November 2026 (the life lot) — a start with an age pyramid.** The project lead, approving the audit of life and population (`content/ai-reserve/review/vivarium/lot-5/audit-vie-population-2026-11-25.md`, proposal 2): "Non, c'est parfait, c'est même incroyable… C'est très très bien ce qu'ils ont suggéré. Je suis partant, on y va." The founders' start of lot 3 C (`people` grown of 18 to 40, all wed the first evening, every reserve full) made 49 to 54 births in the first cycle, fourteen of them on day 8, then left no fertile woman between the founders' fortieth year and their daughters' sixteenth (days 64 to 136). The vivarium's **default start is now a band with an age pyramid** (`--start pyramid`, `vivarium::pyramid`); the founders' start stays (`--start founders`, or implied by `--ages`, `--children` or `--infants`, so the lab's former plans run as before), and the data says which (`meta.settings.start`, `docs/10-vivarium-data.md`). `--people` is the whole band.
    - **The reference band of 25** (ages in age-years): infants of 0.5 (a girl) and 1.5 (a boy); children of 3, 4, 6, 8, 9, 11, 12, 13 and 15; women of 18, 23, 28, 33, 39, 47 and 63; men of 20, 26, 31, 36, 43, 54 and 67. That is 11 under sixteen (44 %) with 2 infants, 12 from sixteen to fifty-nine, 2 past sixty (8 %); 13 women, 12 men. The real data, orders of magnitude from the literature as the audit cites them: the Dobe !Kung (Howell 1979; Lee 1979) and the Hadza (Blurton Jones 2016; Marlowe 2010) count some 40 to 45 % under fifteen and 8 to 10 % past sixty; a birth every 3.2 (Aché, Hill and Hurtado 1996) to 4 (!Kung) real years; husbands a few years older than their wives. A camp of another size takes the roster at even quantiles (50 takes each member twice, 5 takes the members of 3, 11, 20, 33 and 54); under ten or so the shape is too thin to hold.
    - **Kinship.** Each woman from twenty, the youngest first, weds the free grown man nearest three years older than her, ten years either side at most: in the band of 25 the couples 23–26, 28–31, 33–36, 39–43, 47–54 and 63–67, wed at the start whatever their ages (`World::wed_at_start`, kin and one sex barred); the woman of 18 and the man of 20 are unwed and court by the daily rule (D49). Each young one gets a mother whose age at its birth was in the fertile years (D49), whose other children are three years or more apart from it; the wed first, then the one with the fewest children, then the one nearest twenty-six at its birth; her spouse is its father, so the hearth (D58) and the kin bar (D49) work. In the band of 25: the woman of 28 has the children of 0.5, 4 and 8; of 23, 1.5 and 6; of 33, 3, 9 and 13; of 39, 11 and 15; of 47, 12. The infants are in their mothers' arms (D47). The grown are given no parents.
    - **Reserves unequal**: each body but the infants is born with a fat reserve drawn evenly between **45 and 90 %** of full (`World::set_reserve_percent`): the band is born on spring's first day, after a winter; the nursed infants are full. With `--fed` every reserve stays full (the reserve is never drawn on then, a lower one would only bar conception). **A few women with child**: of the wed women of the fertile years who nurse no infant, **three in eight** (rounded) are with child, drawn, 0 to 5 days into a six-day pregnancy (`World::set_pregnant`): one woman in a band of 25, of 33 or of 39 by the seed. (A birth every four years, two of them nursing and nine months with child, leaves a woman who nurses nobody with child some three times in eight.)
    - **Deterministic** (D24): every draw is the vivarium's own integer mix of the run's seed, the tribe, a salt by kind and the member; the roster and the kinship do not depend on the seed.
    - **Decided alone, told:** the roster and every number in it; the pyramid as the default, the old flags still meaning the founders; `--people` as the whole band; the husband nearest three years older, ten at most; women wed from twenty, the youngest grown left unwed; three years between a mother's children; the mother's age at birth looked for first, 26; reserves 45 to 90 %; three eighths with child; full reserves when fed; an infant with no possible mother is not born (never in a band of 25); the grown have no parents (the band remembers none); the lab's page says « personnes par camp » and names the start.

### D42 — The rock under the ground: four rocks in regions, drawn chunk by chunk
- **Reason**: the project lead's yes of 13 November 2026 to the rocks' page (`content/ai-reserve/notes/archive-review/roches/PAGE.md`). What the land offers people at the Stone Age start (rock shelters, caves, boulders, flint, clay, ochre) depends on the rock it stands on: caves only in limestone, flint in chalk and limestone and in the gravel of the rivers crossing them. So the rock comes first, as a layer the others read. **Amended the same day**: the layer is invisible by itself and exists only to drive what players see and use. The project lead: "Changing the ground's tint: I don't see the point. Players won't understand, and we can't dig through the map anyway, so it serves nothing. What we need are clear outcrops of stone outside, with caves, boulders and such. Geological layers are not a bad idea, but they must have a direct impact in the game."
- **Consequences**:
  - **Four rocks**: granite, limestone, chalk, sandstone (`sim::geology::Rock`). Only on worlds made by the generator's version 2 on (D24); a version-1 world has none.
  - **Regions**: a coarse grid of cells (`region_m`, 2 km; a real region is 10 to 100 km, so the game compresses it five to fifty times, a factor named in the content), each with a site anywhere in it and a rock drawn by the content's weights; a tile has the rock of the nearest site, after a shift by a smooth noise of 100 m (`border_cell_m`, up to `border_shift_m` each way) so the borders wander. Two cells of the same rock make one region; a 6 km world shows some six patches of two to four rocks. The values are content, `content/geology.ron` (D27).
  - **By the chunk, never the whole map** (D39): integers only, from the seed, a purpose and absolute coordinates (D24's scheme), so a chunk works out its 1,024 tiles alone from the few sites and noise points around it, the same in any order; held in the world's ground cache like the trees (`ground::Ground::rocks_in`), never stored.
  - **Invisible by itself, in the simulation only**: the rock is never drawn and never sent. The chunk frame carries no rock and the client knows nothing of it (the protocol does not change for it). It is the hidden driver of the visible features to come, each of which gives it its direct impact in the game: bare rock outcrops, boulders, cliffs, rock shelters and caves, flint, quarries. None is built yet.

### D43 — A height per tile travels in the chunks, in decimetres
- **Reason**: the mountain (the project lead's decisions of 13 and 14 November 2026, after the slope drafts on the test branch): the relief is to be **shown by the client from heights**, the slopes read from the difference between neighbouring tiles, never from images of slopes placed by the server. For that the client must know the ground's height under every tile it draws, and the one place the ground travels is the chunk (D08, D26). The project lead's order for building it: "the protocol first, small and clean" (14 November 2026), then the drawing of the slopes, then the heights in the generator.
- **Consequences**:
  - **On the wire** (protocol **v25**): the chunk frame gains a flag (bit 1 of its header, `CHUNK_HAS_HEIGHTS`) and, when it is up, **one `i16` a tile in decimetres above the sea**, in tile order, after the currents and before the trees: 2 KB more a chunk of 32 × 32. Decimetres because a step of a decimetre is below what the eye tells on a slope while a metre is not, and an `i16` of decimetres reaches 3,276 m either way, enough for any world of ours. The flag down, a frame is **byte for byte what it was**: the valley and the game's worlds have no relief yet and send none, so the frames of a version-1 valley still match their pins, and a client that reads v25 draws them as before. The door is unchanged (D26): `protocol` alone writes and reads the heights, the golden frame carries them, `client/net/protocol.gd` alone decodes them.
